\title{An Auditable Theory Compiler for AI-Assisted Formal Mathematics\\
\large Technical Supplement: Finite-Record-Only Safety under Record-Gate Soundness}
\titlerunning{An Auditable Theory Compiler}
\author{Haobo Ma\inst{1} \and Wenlin Zhang\inst{2}}

\begin{abstract}
Formal mathematics projects increasingly use AI agents for drafting,
review, theorem search, and manuscript preparation.  Ungated agent output,
however, can create theorem-looking but content-poor edits, stale source
references, duplicated submission routes, and premature paper promotion.  We
report an auditable theory-to-paper pipeline used around a Lean-backed
BEDC source workspace and an automath publication workspace.  The
pipeline separates source development, intake packets, active paper
directories, AI review loops, deterministic gates, and human promotion
decisions.  Its central rule is that AI output is advisory: load-bearing
mathematical and submission claims must be tied, at the appropriate evidence
level, to source paths, command logs, deterministic checks, and human-approved
promotion.  Under record-gate soundness, the core interface theorem constructs
the finite publication certificates checked by the package verifier from recorded
gate-passage entries and explicitly submitted review surfaces; its safety claim is
deliberately certificate-level and finite-record only.  It proves neither Lean builds,
daemon behaviour, artifact semantics, nor venue acceptance.  The current package
then proves schema-relative finite-record certificate exactness for its six-row,
path-verified instantiation only, while rejecting stronger readings such as external gate
implementation soundness, proof-assistant correctness, artifact semantic validity,
current execution of every gate, or venue acceptance.  Four case
studies illustrate the gates at the path-verified historical-snapshot level: intake
isolation, overlap/submitted blocking, fake-extension rejection, and
artifact-limitation disclosure.
The scoped boundary normal form isolates the five presentation-package weak
points---record-gate soundness, imported source-interface evidence, finite
witness rows, primary-claim inventories, and theorem inventories---and proves that
each supports only its bounded finite-record reading unless a fresh finite upgrade
coordinate is supplied.
\end{abstract}

\section{Problem and contribution}

AI assistance is useful in a formal-mathematics project only if the project can
distinguish proposal from evidence.  A generated theorem statement, a legal
piece of code, a mechanical expansion, or a lower score in a review rubric does
not by itself constitute mathematical discovery.  The practical publication
failure modes are equally structural: a route may overlap a submitted sibling,
an artifact status file may drift from generated counts, or an intake note may
accidentally become a daemon-visible manuscript.

This source is the technical supplement for the presentation-only contribution
\emph{An Auditable Theory Compiler for AI-Assisted Formal Mathematics}, whose
single primary artifact is the two-page source \path{submission_abstract.tex}.
This file is subordinate technical support, not a competing submission title.
The final source-level packaging convention used throughout both sources is the
following: the primary CICM presentation-only article is
\path{submission_abstract.tex}; this file, \path{main.tex}, is the selected
technical supplement; \path{theorem_inventory.json} is the selected bounded
theorem-obligation inventory; and \path{review_bundle/} is the selected exact
supplemental support directory.  No archival replacement is claimed in this
source package.  The short artifact states the workshop-facing thesis and
boundary; this supplement gives the finite-record definitions, theorem proofs,
and case-evidence tables; and the supplemental evidence directory supplies only
the finite records named in the bundle-dependent claims when that exact support
surface is uploaded with matching final digests.  This
file records a compact workflow pattern for avoiding these failures: an auditable
theory-to-paper pipeline in which AI agents propose, review, and triage, while
deterministic gates decide what may be treated as load-bearing evidence.  The
pattern is instantiated in local BEDC and automath workspaces.
The central mathematical/software conclusion of the supplement is a typed
finite-record certificate calculus for publication claims under record-gate
soundness.  Its core chain is Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}
(the finite certificate tuple and gate dispatch),
Definition~\ref{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:record-gate-soundness-external-interface-exactness} (the
standalone record-gate input/output and non-claim boundary),
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table} (the six finite
acceptance predicates), Theorems~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract},
\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, and
\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary} (schema-projection exactness
and non-escalation), Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity} (the typed
record-interface rigidity and refresh fixed-point criterion),
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} (finite
publication-certificate invariants from explicitly supplied record-gate
soundness), Theorem~\ref{thm:external-interface-projection-no-free-upgrade} (the
projection theorem showing that implementation soundness is a separate stronger
reading requiring additional command/source/environment records),
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-record-certificate-rigidity} (the sharp bad-subrecord
normal form for every stronger reading),
Theorem~\ref{thm:boundary-axis-non-escalation-exactness} (simultaneous exactness
of the source-interface, implementation-soundness, artifact-semantic, and
theorem-ledger boundaries), and
Theorem~\ref{thm:named-boundary-positive-coordinate-classification} (the positive
coordinate classification for the concrete boundary issues that otherwise look
like end-to-end workflow claims),
Theorem~\ref{thm:presentation-scoped-boundary-normal-form} (the five-component
presentation-route boundary normal form),
Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-interface-overclaim-skeleton} (the mixed-interface
overclaim skeleton and finite-budget lower bound),
Theorem~\ref{thm:current-boundary-skeleton-non-gluing} (the current package's
non-gluing lower bound for the five over-readings),
Theorem~\ref{thm:current-package-certificate-exactness} (the current package's
schema-relative finite-record instance, not semantic validation of all package
prose or artifacts), and
Theorem~\ref{thm:current-package-extension-coordinate-rigidity} (the exact
absence of current implementation-soundness, fresh-rerun, artifact-semantic, and
venue coordinates), and
Theorem~\ref{thm:manual-ledger-dependence-boundary} (the current package's
manual-ledger dependence and non-completeness boundary), and
Proposition~\ref{prop:final-local-support-state-reconciliation} (the final
four-interface maximality result tying source imports, finite-record safety,
case rows, and theorem-inventory extraction to the same positive-coordinate
upgrade criterion).  Later theoremized statements about bundle shape,
digests, upload conditions, command boundaries, inventories, venue records, and
case snapshots are support-boundary audit claims: they are useful finite-record
consequences, but they are not mathematical invariants of independent interest
and do not replace the printed TeX proofs in the core chain.  For the
presentation version these labels compress to the finite support tuple of
Theorem~\ref{thm:support-surface-invariant-compression}; full label coverage is
carried by \path{theorem_inventory.json}, the theorem-environment extractor log,
the finite record-verifier logs, and the final digest table rather than by
repeating every support-projection theorem in the two-page artifact.  The result is
intentionally not a proof of external implementation-level soundness:
Lean builds, publication-daemon behaviour, artifact semantics, and fresh command
runs enter the argument only when a finite certificate records the corresponding
evidence surface and boundary.  Root-level diagnostic files outside the submitted
primary article, this supplement, and the uploaded-or-equivalent review bundle
are treated below only as local historical or path-verified context unless a
displayed certificate explicitly places the corresponding record on the submitted
support surface.  The CICM presentation-only submission uses the short artifact
plus bibliography; this supplement and \path{review_bundle/} are
evidence-support materials rather than competing primary submissions.

\paragraph{Scope and theoremization boundary.}
The supplement is deliberately limited to auditable theory-to-paper workflow
claims for formal mathematics.  It does not propose a theorem prover, a Lean
hammer, a complete BEDC theory, an automated journal-acceptance method, or a
claim that AI output is proof evidence.  Some logistical facts are stated in
proposition or theorem environments because they are finite-record consequences
used by the certificate calculus; those statements should be read as conditional
audit-record claims over printed paths, tables, manifests, and logs.  The
mathematical obligations are discharged by the displayed TeX definitions,
theorems, and proofs in the core chain, not by the theorem inventory, upload
bookkeeping, digest records, or venue-boundary records.

\begin{proposition}[Final package-shape convention]
\label{prop:final-package-shape-convention}
For the current presentation package there is a single source-level package
shape.  The primary article is \path{submission_abstract.tex}; the selected
technical supplement is \path{main.tex}; the selected bounded theorem-obligation
ledger consists of \path{theorem_inventory.json} and \path{theorem_inventory.md};
and the selected supplemental support surface is the finite tuple
\path{theorem_inventory.json}, \path{theorem_inventory.md}, and exact inclusion
of \path{review_bundle/} with the final digest record
\path{review_bundle/FINAL_DIGESTS_SHA256.md}.  The package records no DOI-bearing
replacement, venue-upload receipt, or alternate supplemental directory.  A
public automath branch/path coordinate, including the coordinate mentioned in
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json} and in
\cite{auditable-pipeline-review-bundle}, is only a candidate archive-equivalent
locator until a finite record confirms byte equality with the final digest table.
Consequently every bundle-backed support claim in this source is accepted
externally only under exact inclusion of that tuple with matching final digests,
or under a later byte-equality record for the candidate branch/path locator;
otherwise it remains a local package-support claim.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The assertion is a finite record comparison.  The primary source names
\path{submission_abstract.tex} as the primary presentation article and states
that \path{main.tex}, \path{theorem_inventory.json},
\path{theorem_inventory.md}, and \path{review_bundle/} are supplemental support
surfaces.  The opening package paragraph of this supplement names the same
coordinates and explicitly selects exact supplemental inclusion unless a later
branch/path archive-equivalent coordinate is accompanied by matching digest
rows.  The digest manifest \path{review_bundle/FINAL_DIGESTS_SHA256.md} records
the finite byte table, while the bundle manifest and bibliography entry record
only a candidate public repository, branch convention, and relative package path.
These finite source records therefore agree on one package shape and on the only
allowed kind of archive-equivalent coordinate: a locator plus byte equality.

It remains only to identify the boundary of the convention.  A source-level
package-shape record fixes which files the manuscript treats as primary,
supplemental, and support material; it does not by itself prove that a venue
upload has occurred.  Hence bundle-backed claims are admissible externally only
after the selected exact support tuple is actually supplied with the digests
listed in the final manifest, or after an archive-equivalent record supplies both
a locator and byte equality with the same digest-identified tuple.  If both
external coordinates are absent, the finite coordinates still exist locally, but
the submitted-support conclusion is not instantiated.  Since the same finite
records contain no DOI, venue-upload receipt, or public byte-equality receipt,
none of those stronger records can be inferred from the source.  This proves both
the single package convention and its conditional external boundary.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nested six-row and seven-row package states]
\label{thm:nested-package-states}
Let $P^{+}_{6}$ denote the current row-frozen certificate state whose accepted
rows are exactly $a_1,\ldots,a_6$ in
Table~\ref{tab:current-acceptance-ledger}.  Let $P^{+}_{7}$ denote the enlarged
submitted support state obtained by adjoining the single theorem-ledger
row $a_7$ whose submitted coordinate is \path{theorem_inventory.json}.  Then
$P^{+}_{6}\subset P^{+}_{7}$ as finite record surfaces, and the inclusion is
conservative for the first six certificates: every acceptance decision, evidence
level, assigned-gate family, discovery-interface field, and command-boundary
field of rows $a_1,\ldots,a_6$ is the same in $P^{+}_{6}$ and $P^{+}_{7}$.  The
only new accepted claim of $P^{+}_{7}$ is the bounded theorem-obligation
ledger claim with syntactic label coverage for $a_7$.  In particular the two states are nested package states,
not competing final ledgers: $P^{+}_{6}$ is the exact six-row current-package
certificate state used by Theorem~\ref{thm:current-package-certificate-exactness},
while $P^{+}_{7}$ is the submitted theorem-ledger extension used by
Theorem~\ref{thm:theorem-obligation-ledger-boundary}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The tables and support records are finite, so the assertion is checked by
coordinates.  By definition, $P^{+}_{6}$ contains the primary article, this
supplement, the six accepted rows $a_1,\ldots,a_6$, their certificate fields, and
the conditional review-bundle support surface named in
Proposition~\ref{prop:current-package-acceptance-certificate-ledger}.  The state
$P^{+}_{7}$ is formed by adding one additional row, $a_7$, together with the
manifest exposure of \path{theorem_inventory.json}.  No row $a_i$ with
$1\leq i\leq 6$ is edited in this construction, and no field of its certificate
is replaced: the same finite tuple $(K_i,\ell_i,E_i,H_i,R_i,D_i,B_i)$ is read by
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table} in both states.  Hence
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} gives the same acceptance decision
for each of the six rows in both states.

The added coordinate has a different declared role.  Its accepted claim is not a
new mathematical proof, a proof-assistant rebuild, or an extractor-completeness
claim; it is the obligation-ledger traceability claim with syntactic label coverage
later classified in Proposition~\ref{prop:manual-theorem-inventory-boundary}.  Since the finite
record added to pass from $P^{+}_{6}$ to $P^{+}_{7}$ is exactly this new row and
its manifest exposure, every accepted claim outside $a_7$ is inherited unchanged
from $P^{+}_{6}$, while $a_7$ supplies only the bounded theorem-ledger coordinate.
This proves both the nesting and the non-competition statement.
\end{proof}

The setting is adjacent to proof-assistant systems such as Lean
\cite{deMouraKADR2015Lean}, machine-learning interfaces to formal proving such
as LeanDojo \cite{YangEtAl2023LeanDojo}, informal-to-formal guidance methods
such as Draft--Sketch--Prove \cite{JiangEtAl2023DraftSketchProve}, and
large-scale formal-proof archives such as the Archive of Formal Proofs
\cite{BlanchetteHMN2015AFP}.  Our focus is different: we study the
publication-facing evidence discipline around such sources.

\section{Architecture}

The workflow separates source work from publication work, and the separation is
the organizing contract for the rest of the supplement.  BEDC source files are
treated as finite-kernel theory together with proof obligations and separate
implementation contracts.  Lean and related markers are formal-verification
records for named obligations, not object-language closure by themselves.
Finite manifests, evaluator rows, bundled snapshots, and command logs are the
substrate witnesses from which publication certificates are built.  AI agents
remain advisory: they may propose, review, and triage, but they do not promote
claims into accepted theory.  The automath layer is therefore a publication
compiler: it packages the source-theory interface, finite witnesses, route state,
and submission boundary without merging those evidence axes.

\begin{definition}[Architecture-axis map]
\label{def:architecture-axis-map}
The compiler architecture uses five disjoint evidence axes.  An accepted claim may
read evidence from an axis only at the admissible evidence level shown in
the displayed axis map; the negative boundary in the last column
is part of the claim certificate and cannot be discharged by prose from another
axis.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{p{.16\textwidth}p{.27\textwidth}p{.27\textwidth}X}
\toprule
Axis & Role in this paper & Admissible evidence & Negative boundary \\
\midrule
BEDC source theory & finite-kernel theory, proof obligations, and implementation
contracts & pinned source paths, commits, imported labels, bounded summaries, and
source-interface records & not a publication acceptance record, not a fresh rebuild,
and not a complete BEDC exposition \\
Lean markers & formal-verification records for named obligations & recorded build,
axiom-purity, marker-existence, or source-interface rows when named by the package &
not object-language closure, not discovery by itself, and not proof of unrelated
claims \\
Finite witnesses & manifest/evaluator substrate evidence for artifacts and package
certificates & manifest rows, evaluator outcomes, bundled snapshots, verifier logs,
file digests, and limitation ledgers & not semantic universality, not whole-tree
dynamic validation, and not an unbounded artifact rerun \\
Agents & proposal, search, review, ranking, and repair assistance & advisory drafts,
review reports, scheduler scores, and gate-input suggestions after being recorded
as such & not proof evidence, not human promotion, not novelty certification, and
not gate passage \\
Automath publication compiler & intake, route, overlap, submission-pack, and support
surface governance & promotion records, active-paper markers, overlap ledgers,
submission-interface maps, digest manifests, command logs, and upload-or-archive
records when present & not the whole mathematical contribution, not venue
acceptance, and not authority to merge evidence axes \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Axis non-escalation]
\label{prop:architecture-axis-non-escalation}
In any record-gate-sound package using Definition~\ref{def:architecture-axis-map},
passage or evidence on one architecture axis cannot certify a load-bearing claim
whose claim kind requires another axis.  More precisely, if a claim certificate
uses an axis $A$ but its stronger reading needs an evidence item or negative-boundary
discharge listed only on a different axis $A'$, then the stronger reading is not
accepted unless the package contains a separate submitted coordinate for $A'$ and
the gates assigned to that coordinate pass.  Consequently
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} constructs only finite publication
certificates whose axis coordinates are explicitly present in the submitted support
surface; it does not infer BEDC closure from Lean markers, artifact semantics from
manifest rows, proof evidence from agents, venue upload from automath route state,
or support-surface availability from local bundle paths.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The operational interface records a claim by finite coordinates: claim kind,
evidence surface, evidence level, pass records assigned by $\Gamma$, and the
boundary field.  Definition~\ref{def:architecture-axis-map} makes the axis of each
such coordinate explicit and declares the corresponding negative boundary.  If a
stronger reading needs an item on a different axis $A'$, then either the claim-kind
coordinate does not name the kind requiring $A'$, the evidence surface does not
contain an admissible $A'$ item, a required $A'$ pass record is missing, or the
boundary field still excludes the stronger reading.  In all four cases the finite
record verifier of Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier}
rejects the stronger reading: the dispatch table cannot assign an unmentioned gate,
the surface check cannot read an unsubmitted path or log, the pass-record check
cannot be satisfied without the row, and the boundary check preserves the declared
negative boundary.

Conversely, when a separate coordinate for $A'$ is submitted, it is just another
finite component of the package.  It can be accepted only by the same schema:
its claim kind must dispatch to the appropriate gates, its evidence surface must
list the admissible $A'$ evidence, and its boundary must state exactly what is and
is not certified.  Therefore evidence never moves between axes by paraphrase or by
agent-generated text; it moves only by adding an explicit finite coordinate and
passing the assigned gates.  Applying this to the five rows of
Definition~\ref{def:architecture-axis-map} gives the listed consequences.  They are
also the axis-level instance of the coordinate-determination result later isolated
in Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}: facts outside the
finite coordinates of the accepted package are not conclusions of the certificate.
\end{proof}
The reader path is therefore architectural before it is logistical.  The rest of
the supplement first fixes the source-interface spine, then defines the finite
record verifier, then proves the certificate construction and non-escalation
boundary, then instantiates that boundary on the current six-row package, and
only then lists the local case snapshots and command-run inventory.  File tables
are included as finite witnesses for this interface, not as a substitute for the
compiler architecture.

Concretely, the newmath source workspace contains Lean files, scripts, and
artifact sources.  The automath publication workspace contains intake packets,
active paper tracks, review state, and submission metadata.  A candidate first
enters an intake seed.  It becomes an active paper only after a human promotion
decision creates a \texttt{2026\_*} directory with \texttt{main.tex} and
\texttt{PIPELINE.md}.  This prevents source organization from being mistaken for
a submission route.

The source side supplies a discovery-gate calculus.  In this calculus,
statement-only and code-only candidates are insufficient; mechanical expansion
and compression are not discovery by themselves; discovery claims require a
semantic certificate and a ledger witness; demotion preserves source-interface
audit soundness;
accepted claims must form an auditable lineage DAG; and a pipeline is safe only
when discovery claims pass the gate before being recorded as accepted work.

The exact source interface used by the proof below was extracted from the pinned
commit on 2026-06-01.  At lines 238--246, the source states the DNA-completeness
obligation for gate-passed discovery genomes.  At lines 248--255, it states that
candidates supplying only a statement locus or only legal theorem code do not
pass the discovery gate as theorem genomes.  At lines 258--267 and 269--278, it
states that mechanical expansion and same-classifier genome compression do not
imply discovery.  At lines 280--297, it states that a discovery transition
supplies a semantic naming certificate and a ledger witness.  At lines 299--332,
it records the positive-discovery, public-weight, and gate-kind-soundness
interfaces used by the package verifier below.  At lines 334--345, it states
that gate demotion leaves a claim kind no stronger than its actual evidence.  At
lines 366--374, it states that lineage-consistent accepted claims form an
auditable acyclic lineage graph.  At lines 393--418, it states that the lowest
score need not be the best target and that, when all other discovery-potential
coordinates are fixed, a positive classifier-change coefficient makes the
selection operator favor the classifier-changing candidate.  At lines 432--439,
it states that an accepted pipeline has no accepted discovery claim missing gate
evidence.  At lines 441--455, pipeline safety states that accepted discovery
claims have classifier change, semantic naming certificate, ledger witness,
DNA-complete theorem genome, score certificate, lineage witness, and an explicit
not-claimed boundary.  At lines 457--485, the main pipeline theorem and principle
separate record growth, score reduction, recombination, classifier change,
ledgered discovery, and certified structural theorem-genome change.  These paraphrases are
used only as a stable interface to the pinned source theorem spine; no stronger
source rebuild claim is made here.

\begin{lemma}[Manual bounded source-interface extraction correctness]
\label{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}
For the pinned newmath repository path
\path{papers/bedc/parts/visions/audit_map_methodology/automated_theory_discovery_pipeline_calculus.tex}
at commit \path{3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b},
Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record} is the complete imported interface used
by this paper at the manual path-verified level and only at that level.  Its extraction certificate is:
manual line-range audit against the pinned file, mirrored in
\path{source_interface_record.json}, extraction date 2026-06-01, and summary
mode ``bounded paraphrase'' rather than replacement of the full source
statements.  Consequently every use below of an imported discovery-gate label is
limited to the corresponding row's path, commit, label, line range, and bounded
summary.  Even when an explicitly named executable finite record comparison is
also invoked, the upgraded content is only equality and containment of those
finite fields; exact source statements are not imported.  In particular, the
lemma is not a fresh Lean/BEDC rebuild, not an axiom-purity certificate, and not
a semantic proof of the imported source theorem statements.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first two columns of Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record} give, for every
imported label named in the source-binding record, the source label and the line
range from which the interface is extracted.  The paragraph immediately before
the table fixes the only source file and commit used for those labels; the
paragraph immediately after the table fixes the extraction date and identifies
\path{source_interface_record.json} as the mirrored check record.  The third
column deliberately records bounded summaries: each row states only the
interface property needed later, such as insufficiency of statement-only records,
certificate-and-ledger requirements, demotion soundness, lineage acyclicity,
bounded classifier-change selection, or pipeline safety.  No later theorem cites
an imported label outside this sixteen-row list, and no later proof uses a
stronger form than the corresponding bounded summary.  A Lean rebuild,
axiom-purity check, exact source-statement import, or semantic source-theorem
validation would require additional command-result or formal-source coordinates;
none is among the fields read in this lemma.  Thus the table is complete for the
imported interface of this paper and correct at the stated evidence level: exact
as to path, commit, label, line range, method, date, and summary mode, and
bounded as to mathematical restatement.\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Manual source-interface non-escalation]
\label{prop:manual-source-interface-non-escalation}
Let $I$ be the imported discovery-gate interface certified by
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}.  Any later use of an
imported source label in this paper is a consequence of $I$ only if it is a
function of the finite fields recorded in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}:
the source path, pinned commit, label, line range, extraction date, bounded
summary, and evidence-level qualifier.  A proposed stronger use---for example an
exact import of the original source theorem statement, a fresh BEDC or Lean
rebuild, an axiom-purity statement, or a semantic validation of the imported
source theorem---is accepted by the finite-record safety interface only after the
submitted record surface is enlarged by the corresponding source-statement,
formal-build, or command-run coordinate and by the assigned pass records for that
coordinate.  Without that enlargement the stronger use is rejected as a finite
bad subrecord and the accepted consequence remains the manual path-verified
bounded interface.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness} identifies exactly the
finite fields in $I$ and states that the summaries are bounded paraphrases rather
than replacements of the original source statements.  Therefore two possible
review states that agree on those fields but differ in an unsubmitted source
checkout, proof-assistant state, axiom audit, or executable extraction log give
the same values to every coordinate read by $I$.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}, no claim depending on
one of the differing unsubmitted coordinates is a consequence of the finite
interface record.

If a stronger use is made load-bearing, then it strictly enlarges the original
source-interface reading in the stronger-reading preorder of
Definition~\ref{def:stronger-reading-preorder}: exact source-statement import
adds an evidence-surface coordinate, a BEDC or Lean rebuild adds formal-source or
command-run coordinates, axiom-purity adds an assigned gate and pass record, and
semantic validation adds a new claim-kind or boundary coordinate.  The normal
form of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form} says that such a
genuine strengthening is accepted exactly when the added finite coordinates and
their gate records are supplied.  If they are absent,
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton} returns the missing
coordinate as a finite bad subrecord.  Thus the source import used downstream,
including in Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}, remains the manual
path-verified bounded interface and nothing stronger.\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Reviewable source-snapshot interface]
\label{prop:reviewable-source-snapshot-interface}
The imported discovery-gate spine used in
the finite-record interface theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}, has a reviewer-facing source
interface.  Namely, the tuple consisting of the pinned local source path, commit
\path{3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b},
Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}, \path{source_interface_record.json},
and the supplemental copy
\path{review_bundle/source_snapshots/automated_theory_discovery_pipeline_calculus_3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b.tex}
is sufficient to inspect every imported source label used by this paper at the
path-verified evidence level.  The distribution mode for review is supplemental
upload of \path{review_bundle/} with the presentation-only submission.  No
public archive URL or DOI is part of the current theorem interface.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness} proves that the only
imported source labels used by the paper are the sixteen rows of
Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}, each with a fixed line range and bounded
summary.  The paragraph preceding that lemma fixes the original local source
path, source commit, extraction date, and JSON mirror.  The supplemental copy is
a file-level snapshot of the same source-interface document, named by the same
commit hash.  Therefore a reviewer does not need access to the author's absolute
path in order to inspect the imported interface: the table gives the labels and
line ranges, the JSON record gives the machine-readable binding, and the bundle
copy gives the text snapshot against which those ranges are checked.  The current reviewer-facing distribution mode is fixed by the proposition:
the bundle is uploadable supplemental material, not an unrecorded private
dependency, and no archival identifier is needed for the bounded interface
claim.\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Executable source-interface record comparison]
\label{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency}
The source-interface table, its JSON mirror, and the bundled source snapshot give
one record-level imported discovery-gate interface for
the finite-record interface theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}.  The record fields are as
follows: the source commit is
\path{3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b}; the bundled snapshot is
\path{review_bundle/source_snapshots/automated_theory_discovery_pipeline_calculus_3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b.tex};
the finite comparison script is
\path{review_bundle/verify_source_interface_record.py}; the recorded successful
comparison log is \path{review_bundle/source_interface_verification_run.log};
the recorded SHA-256 digest checked by the script is
\path{a492900222dd66d154cdd5a79d2b2ed0b856ad1ec59e5dfce55dbc21875ed1b2};
the recorded snapshot length is 498 lines; for each of the sixteen line ranges in
\path{source_interface_record.json}, the corresponding imported label is recorded
inside the stated snapshot range; and the sixteen triples \emph{label, line range,
bounded summary} in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record} are the sixteen JSON
records.  The verifier log uses two distinct commit fields: the imported external
newmath source commit is the pinned commit displayed above, while the local
publication-package commit is the Git \path{HEAD} of this paper workspace at the
time the comparison command is run.  Hence, at the finite record-comparison level, the manuscript table,
JSON record, review-bundle manifest, digest, line count, and bundled snapshot
define one consistent imported-source interface.  The comparison does not assert
semantic truth of the imported source theorems, does not perform a BEDC/Lean
source-theory extraction, does not reproduce the exact source theorem statements
as manuscript theorems, and does not replace the bounded paraphrase discipline of
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}.  Exact source statement
inspection, if desired by a reviewer, is supported only by the bundled snapshot
and line ranges; it is not an imported theorem obligation of this paper.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proposition is a finite equality-and-containment check over submitted
records.  The script \path{review_bundle/verify_source_interface_record.py}
parses the sixteen rows of Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record} from this source,
loads \path{source_interface_record.json}, loads the
\path{pinned_source_snapshot} object of
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json}, and computes the SHA-256 digest
and line count of the bundled snapshot.  It then checks seven finite conditions:
the ordered manuscript rows are the ordered JSON records; the JSON source path
and commit equal the manifest's original path and commit; the ordered JSON line
ranges equal the manifest's \path{line_ranges_used}; the computed digest equals
the manifest digest; the computed line count equals the manifest line count; and for each declared range,
the corresponding \path{\label{...}} token occurs inside that inclusive snapshot
interval.

The recorded run in \path{review_bundle/source_interface_verification_run.log}
has status \path{pass}.  Its counts are sixteen manuscript table rows, sixteen JSON
records, sixteen manifest ranges, snapshot line count 498, and digest
\path{a492900222dd66d154cdd5a79d2b2ed0b856ad1ec59e5dfce55dbc21875ed1b2}.  Its
\path{imported_newmath_source_commit} field is the external source commit
\path{3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b}; its
\path{local_publication_package_commit} field is only provenance for the local
paper checkout used to run the verifier and is not a second imported-source pin.  Its
per-row label checks all report \path{label_in_range: true} for the sixteen labels
listed in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}.  Therefore no accepted row can
be a table-only, JSON-only, manifest-only, or out-of-range source-interface row:
any such defect would violate one of the seven checked finite conditions and would
appear as a finite bad subrecord.  What remains unchecked is exactly outside the
claim: the script does not interpret or prove the imported theorem statements,
does not check BEDC semantic extraction, and does not check Lean or source-theory
completeness.  The manuscript does not quote the imported statements as exact
source theorem text.  The exact source text remains available only as the bundled
snapshot at the displayed line ranges.  Thus the executable comparison strengthens
the earlier manual audit into a reproducible equality-and-containment certificate
while preserving the bounded-paraphrase and non-semantic boundary.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bounded source-interface maximality]
\label{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality}
Let $I_{\rm man}$ be the manual imported-source interface of
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}, and let $I_{\rm exe}$
be the executable finite comparison of
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency}.  In the
finite-record safety interface of Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface},
the strongest imported discovery-gate premise available from
$I_{\rm man}$ together with $I_{\rm exe}$ is exactly the following finite
certificate:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the imported source path and pinned source commit;
\item the sixteen ordered labels, line ranges, extraction date, and bounded
statement summaries in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record};
\item equality of those ordered triples with \path{source_interface_record.json};
\item agreement with the review-bundle manifest on source path, source commit,
line ranges, snapshot digest, and snapshot line count; and
\item containment of each imported label token in its declared bundled-snapshot
line interval.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently every accepted use of an imported discovery-gate label in the main
theorem chain, in particular in Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface},
factors through this finite certificate.  Any reading that imports the exact
source theorem statements, asserts semantic truth of the imported theorems,
performs a BEDC or Lean rebuild, proves axiom purity, or certifies source-theory
completeness is a properly stronger reading and is rejected unless the submitted
surface is enlarged by the corresponding finite source-statement, command-run,
formal-build, axiom-audit, or semantic-check coordinate and by its assigned pass
records.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness} supplies exactly the
manual finite fields in (1) and (2), and it states that the row summaries are
bounded paraphrases rather than replacements for the full source statements.
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency} supplies exactly
the executable finite fields in (3)--(5): the script compares the manuscript
table, JSON mirror, bundle manifest, digest, line count, and labelled intervals
and reports a finite bad subrecord on any failed comparison.  These two records
therefore have the displayed union as their complete positive content.

The verifier used in Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} can read only
submitted finite coordinates.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:manual-source-interface-non-escalation},
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-evidence-level-separation}, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}, two review states
that agree on the displayed finite certificate but differ in an unsubmitted
source checkout, proof-assistant state, axiom audit, semantic extractor, or build
log are indistinguishable to the imported-source component of the certificate
constructor.  Hence every accepted imported-label premise in the theorem chain
factors through the displayed certificate.

Each excluded reading strictly adds a finite coordinate in the preorder of
Definition~\ref{def:stronger-reading-preorder}: exact source-statement import
adds a source-text evidence coordinate; BEDC or Lean rebuilding adds a formal
source or command-run coordinate; axiom purity adds an audit coordinate and pass
row; and semantic source-theorem validation adds a semantic-check coordinate.
The finite extension-budget normal form,
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form}, accepts such a stronger
reading exactly when those added coordinates and pass records are present.
When they are absent, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}
classifies the missing coordinate as a finite bad subrecord.  Thus the displayed
certificate is maximal for the submitted source-interface surface while remaining
strictly bounded to record equality and label containment.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Public-source reachability non-escalation]
\label{thm:public-source-reachability-non-escalation}
In the current source package, the citation \cite{bedc-newmath-source} and the
commit string \path{3fb3d6a0641767388a401883062aa522ea0b397b} serve only as the
public-source coordinate for the imported newmath workspace.  The load-bearing
source-interface premise used by Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}
remains the bundled finite record certified by
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality}: path, commit string,
sixteen labels, line ranges, bounded summaries, JSON equality, manifest agreement,
snapshot digest and line count, and label containment in the bundled snapshot.
Unless the submitted surface additionally contains a dated public-repository
reachability and byte-equality log for the pinned commit and bundled snapshot, no
claim in this paper may infer that the public repository was reachable at review
time, that the public branch contains the pinned commit, or that public bytes are
equal to the bundled snapshot bytes.  If such a dated log is added, its only
upgrade is the finite reachability/equality coordinate named by that log; it still
does not prove semantic truth of the imported source theorems, Lean/BEDC rebuild
success, marker correctness, or axiom purity.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality} identifies the complete
positive imported-source content currently supplied to the finite-record safety
theorem: it is a finite bundle-backed equality-and-containment certificate over
the manuscript table, \path{source_interface_record.json}, the review-bundle
manifest, and the bundled source snapshot.  None of those fields is a dated
network observation.  In particular, a commit string in a citation and a local
snapshot digest are not fields recording a public-repository command, remote URL,
environment, exit code, log path, fetched object identity, or byte comparison
against a public checkout.

Consider two review states that agree on every field listed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality} and on the bibliography
entry \cite{bedc-newmath-source}.  In the first state a reviewer also has a dated
successful remote-reachability and byte-equality log; in the second state that log
is absent.  The current verifier and theorem chain read the same finite
coordinates in both states, because the distinguishing public-reachability log is
not part of the submitted support surface.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}, a stronger conclusion
that distinguishes these states cannot be inferred from the common finite tuple.
Thus public reachability, public commit containment, and public byte equality are
not current consequences of the citation or bundled snapshot records.

If a later package adds the dated log, then the support tuple has a new finite
coordinate: the command or check performed, the public repository locator, the
source commit, the compared path or object, the environment, the exit code, the
log path, and the resulting digest or equality record.  The finite extension is
accepted exactly as an added coordinate under
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form}; it does not add any of the
separate semantic, formal-build, marker, or axiom-audit coordinates excluded by
Theorem~\ref{thm:source-interface-semantic-completeness-obstruction}.  Hence the
only possible upgrade is the stated finite public reachability/equality reading.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Source-interface semantic-completeness obstruction]
\label{thm:source-interface-semantic-completeness-obstruction}
Under the source-interface surface certified by
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality}, every accepted imported
discovery-gate premise factors through the five finite fields listed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality}.  In particular, no theorem
in the main publication-safety chain may infer semantic completeness of the
imported source theory, correctness of the bounded summaries as exact source
statements, Lean/BEDC rebuild success, marker correctness, or axiom purity from
that surface.  A package claim with any one of those stronger meanings is
accepted by the same finite-record calculus if and only if the support surface is
extended by a nonempty machine-readable source-interface coordinate recording at
least the relevant source file, source commit, extractor or checker command,
runtime environment, exit code, log path, produced record, digest binding, and an
assigned pass row for the strengthened claim kind.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $F(I)$ denote the finite projection consisting of the five fields displayed
in Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded-source-interface-maximality}.  That theorem proves
that $F(I)$ is the strongest positive imported-source premise available to
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} from the submitted manual and
executable source-interface records.  Therefore any accepted consequence using
an imported discovery-gate label is a function of $F(I)$.

Now consider any stronger semantic reading, for instance exact source-statement
import or marker correctness.  There are two possible review states with the
same $F(I)$: in both states the path, commit, labels, line ranges, bounded
summaries, JSON equality, bundle-manifest agreement, digest row, line count, and
label containment checks agree; in one state an additional semantic extractor or
proof checker succeeds, while in the other no such command has been submitted or
the unsubmitted semantic fact is false.  The finite source-interface verifier
cannot distinguish these states because the distinguishing coordinate is not in
$F(I)$.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:manual-source-interface-non-escalation}, the stronger
semantic conclusion is therefore not a consequence of the current interface.

Conversely, if the support surface is enlarged by the displayed finite
machine-readable semantic coordinate and by its assigned pass row, the enlarged
coordinate is no longer invisible to the certificate calculus: it becomes an
ordinary finite extension of the tuple read by the verifier.  The acceptance of
the strengthened source-interface reading is then exactly the fixed-point case
of Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity}, applied to the enlarged support
surface.  Because at least one new command-result, digest-binding, or pass-row
field is added, the extension is nonempty.  This proves both the obstruction and
the stated if-and-only-if upgrade criterion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Source-interface evidence-level separation]
\label{prop:source-interface-evidence-level-separation}
Let $r$ be any accepted manuscript claim whose discovery component cites one of
the imported labels in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}.  If $r$ uses only
Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}, then the imported-label
premise of $r$ is accepted only at the manual path-verified bounded-paraphrase
level.  If $r$ additionally cites
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency}, then the same
premise is upgraded only by the finite record comparisons listed in that
proposition: table--JSON equality, manifest agreement, snapshot digest and line
count agreement, and label containment in the declared ranges.  In neither case
does the premise become a fresh Lean/BEDC rebuild, a proof of the semantic truth
of the imported source theorem, or an unbounded restatement of the source text.
Consequently the finite-record interface theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}, can use the imported
discovery-gate spine only through these two explicitly separated evidence
levels.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The operational evidence level of a cited source-interface premise is determined
by the record fields actually available for that premise.  Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness}
supplies a pinned path, commit, extraction date, label, line range, and bounded
summary for each of the ten imported labels, and its proof states that no later
use may exceed that bounded summary.  Therefore a claim using only the lemma has
exactly the manual path-verified bounded-paraphrase evidence listed there.

Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency} adds a different
finite record: the verifier parses the manuscript table, the JSON mirror, the
manifest's pinned-source-snapshot record, and the bundled source snapshot, and it
checks the six finite conditions enumerated in its proof.  Those checks upgrade
the source-interface premise only with respect to record-file consistency and
range containment.  The same proposition expressly excludes semantic truth of
the imported theorems and preserves the bounded-paraphrase restriction.  No row
in Table~\ref{tab:source-interface-record}, no field of
\path{source_interface_record.json}, and no command-boundary row records a fresh
Lean/BEDC rebuild or a semantic source-theorem verifier.  Hence an imported-label
premise has exactly the two evidence levels stated in the proposition and no
stronger one.

The finite-record interface theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}, requires a discovery-gate source
interface with recorded evidence surface and boundary.  Applying the preceding
paragraphs to each of its imported discovery-gate citations gives precisely the
manual path-verified interface, optionally strengthened by the finite executable
record comparison, and forbids promotion of that interface into an unrecorded
semantic or rebuild claim.\end{proof}

\section{Operational interface}

\paragraph{Reader map.}
The supplement separates one central theorem chain from support-boundary audit
records.  The core finite-record safety argument is:
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface} fixes the certificate tuple,
claim kinds, gate dispatch, verifier predicate, evidence levels, and
record-gate-soundness hypothesis;
Definition~\ref{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface} isolates that
hypothesis as an external finite input/output interface, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:record-gate-soundness-external-interface-exactness} proves
that it has no hidden semantic or venue-acceptance content;
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table} restates the finite
acceptance predicates as the table relation \(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}\);
Theorems~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract},
\ref{thm:finite-verifier-normal-form},
\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, and
\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary} prove verifier exactness and
non-escalation from those predicates;
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity} gives the record-interface rigidity
and fixed-point refresh criterion that turns these finite projections into a
typed interface specification rather than a count of unpacking lemmas;
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} constructs finite publication
certificates under record-gate soundness; and
Theorem~\ref{thm:current-package-certificate-exactness} specializes the calculus
to the current six-row package.  The later results about packaging, upload
conditions, digests, command runs, inventories, venue records, and case snapshots
are deliberately theoremized audit-record consequences of finite rows and tables.
They document the support surface and its negative boundary; they should not be
read as independent mathematical invariants or as substitutes for the printed
TeX proofs in the core chain.

\subsection{Abstract finite-record interface}

For this long technical supplement and its two-page CICM presentation artifact,
the following finite interface is the self-contained formal block used by every
later safety theorem.  It fixes, before use, the certificate tuple
\(\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\), the dispatch table \(\Gamma\), the finite
verifier predicate \(V\), the acceptance relation \(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}\), the
record-gate-soundness predicate \(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\), and the
stronger-reading preorder \(\preceq_{\Gamma}\).  All hypotheses in the headline
safety theorem are finite-record hypotheses over these objects; none is supplied
by a hidden semantic oracle, by the manual theorem inventory, or by generated
prose.

\begin{definition}[Operational acceptance interface]
\label{def:operational-acceptance-interface}
A \emph{claim} is a manuscript assertion that can affect a mathematical,
artifact, route, or submission decision.  A \emph{load-bearing claim} is a claim
whose removal would change a theorem statement, a case-study conclusion, or the
submission-readiness status.  An \emph{advisory output} is any AI-agent draft,
score, suggested theorem, review packet, or repair proposal before a gate records
it.  A \emph{deterministic gate} is a repeatable predicate, script, or checklist
whose inputs and pass/fail decision are recorded independently of the prose
generated by an agent.  A \emph{human promotion} is the explicit editorial act
that moves a candidate into publication state, for example by creating an active
\texttt{2026\_*} paper directory with \texttt{main.tex} and \texttt{PIPELINE.md}.
An \emph{evidence surface} is the set of source paths, commits, commands, logs,
inventories, and witness records cited for a claim.  An \emph{acceptance entry}
is a manuscript or submission-ledger record that marks a candidate claim as
usable in the publication-facing argument.  \emph{Accepted work} is the subset of
candidate claims with such an entry.  This is a status notion only: the
definition records that an editorial acceptance entry exists, but it does not by
itself assert external editorial acceptance or implementation correctness.  A
pipeline is \emph{record-gate-sound for acceptance} when each acceptance entry records
the claim kind, the evidence surface, the evidence level, and a pass record for
every gate in $\Gamma$ assigned to that claim kind, and when a route-boundary
entry also records human promotion.

The deterministic dispatch function $\Gamma$ from claim kinds to gates is part
of the operational interface.  Its domain is
\[
\{\text{formal-source},\text{ traceability},\text{ route},
\text{ submission},\text{ artifact},\text{ discovery},\text{ work-selection}\}.
\]
It sends formal-source claims to the build and axiom-purity gates; traceability
claims to inventory and marker-existence gates; route claims to promotion,
overlap, and submission-state gates; submission claims to submission-pack gates;
artifact claims to status and limitation-ledger gates; discovery claims to the
pinned discovery gate above and, for extension claims, to the theorem-content
delta gate; and work-selection claims to the scheduler gate, whose evidence
surface is a critical-path score or backlog window together with the claim kind
that the selected item is meant to advance.  For mixed claims, $\Gamma$ returns
the union of the gate sets for the claim kinds present.

The route gates have separate operational meanings.  The \emph{promotion gate}
uses the active-paper evidence surface, namely a human promotion act producing a
\path{2026_*} directory with \path{main.tex} and \path{PIPELINE.md}; passing
means the candidate may be daemon-visible publication work, and failing means it
remains intake or source material.  The \emph{overlap gate} uses route identity,
semantic-overlap ledgers, submitted-sibling paths, and program-board state;
passing means advancement is route-disjoint or has a recorded resolution, and
failing means advancement is blocked until closure, merge, supersession, or
waiting for prior feedback.  The \emph{submission-state gate} uses submission
markers, venue state, board state, and submission-pack metadata; passing means
the route's publication state permits the proposed action, and failing means the
action would duplicate, race, or under-specify an active or submitted route.  The
\emph{theorem-content delta gate} uses theorem inventories, hard-lint output, and
delta records such as \path{ARTIFACT_INVENTORY.md}; passing means that an
extension claim identifies load-bearing mathematical content, and failing means
that compilation, file churn, or surface edits may not be reported as substantive
theorem growth.

A \emph{reviewable artifact} for this paper is either a public repository file,
a supplemental file included with the submission, or an explicit
historical-evidence boundary stating that the local path is used only as
path-verified evidence and not as a fresh command-run claim.  A \emph{case
witness record} is path-complete when its evidence surface gives a
concrete source or artifact path for each load-bearing component of the row.  A
route-overlap witness has the fields \emph{route identity}, \emph{submitted
sibling}, \emph{overlap reason}, \emph{gate decision}, and \emph{allowed next
actions}.  An artifact-limitation witness has the fields \emph{artifact status
path}, \emph{limitation ledger path}, \emph{observed limitation}, \emph{gate
decision}, and \emph{allowed manuscript claim}.  A promotion witness has the
fields \emph{candidate surface}, \emph{human promotion evidence},
\emph{active-paper markers}, \emph{gate decision}, and \emph{allowed manuscript
claim}.  A theorem-content witness has the fields \emph{changed surface},
\emph{theorem inventory or hard-lint surface}, \emph{substantive-content delta},
\emph{gate decision}, and \emph{allowed manuscript claim}.  The inventory files
\path{ARTIFACT_INVENTORY.md}, \path{SOURCE_MAP.md}, and
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json} are part of the evidence surface
for this paper and give the compact path catalogue used by
Table~\ref{tab:case-witnesses}.

We distinguish two evidence levels.  A claim is \emph{path-verified} when this
paper records the relevant source path, artifact path, pinned commit or local
publication path, and gate decision, but does not assert that a command was
freshly rerun for this submission.  A claim is \emph{command-run} when the paper
also records the command, source commit, environment, exit code, and log path
for a fresh run.  This long technical supplement uses path-verified architecture
and case evidence unless explicitly marked command-run.

A uniform \emph{pass record} is the finite tuple
\[
\rho=(g,I,d,e,\ell),
\]
where $g$ is the gate name, $I$ is the input surface submitted to that gate,
$d$ is the recorded decision, $e$ is the evidence pointer supporting the
decision, and $\ell$ is the evidence level.  A pass record is assigned to a
claim certificate only when its evidence pointer is contained in the claim's
evidence surface, in a submitted manifest or table row, in a bundled snapshot, or
in an explicit historical-evidence boundary.

A \emph{publication certificate} for a load-bearing claim $c$ is the finite
record
\[
\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B).
\]
Here $K$ is the nonempty finite set of claim kinds carried by $c$, $\ell$ is the
evidence level, $E$ is the evidence surface, $H$ is either a human-promotion
record or the marker that $c$ does not cross the intake-to-publication boundary,
$R$ gives one pass record, with the five fields above, for each gate in
$\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$, $D$ is present exactly when $K$ contains discovery
and then names the pinned discovery-gate source-interface rows used for the
claim, and $B$ records either the command-run fields or the explicit boundary
that no command-run assertion is made.  A certificate is \emph{locally
checkable} when these finite fields are present and every path, manifest row, or
log named by a field lies in the submitted artifact, the supplemental review
bundle, or the explicit path-verified historical boundary.

For later use we make the local check explicit as a finite verifier.  The
concrete package checker supplied with the review bundle is
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}.  Its positive invocation is
\path{python review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}; it reads exactly
\path{review_bundle/certificate_schema.json},
\path{review_bundle/current_package_pass_records.json},
\path{review_bundle/submission_interface_map.json},
\path{review_bundle/primary_claim_inventory.json}, and
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json}, and writes the recorded pass log
\path{review_bundle/certificate_verification_run.log}.  Its negative-example
invocation is
\path{python review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py --negative-example review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example.json},
with recorded log \path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example_run.md};
that fixture upgrades a route row to command-run strength while omitting the
command-run tuple and is rejected with the finite bad subrecord
\path{neg1: command-run fields incomplete}.  The mathematical verifier below is
the schema abstraction of this package checker.

Let \(\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)\) be the seven-kind domain and let
\(\mathsf{Lev}=\{\mathsf{path},\mathsf{command}\}\).  For a candidate record
\(\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\), define six finite predicates:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$P_1$:] $K\ne\emptyset$, $K\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$, and
$\ell\in\mathsf{Lev}$.
\item[$P_2$:] $E$ is finite and each item of $E$ is a submitted path,
review-bundle path, manifest row, log path, pinned commit, or explicit
historical-evidence boundary.
\item[$P_3$:] for every gate $g\in\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$, $R$ contains a
pass record $(g,I,d,e,\ell')$ whose evidence pointer lies in $E$ or in a named
submitted table, manifest row, bundled snapshot, or historical boundary.
\item[$P_4$:] $H$ records the human promotion exactly when $c$ crosses the
intake-to-publication boundary and otherwise records that no such boundary is
crossed.
\item[$P_5$:] $D$ is present exactly when discovery lies in $K$, and then names
the pinned discovery-gate source-interface rows used by the claim.
\item[$P_6$:] $B$ either records command, source commit, environment, exit code,
and log path for command-run evidence or explicitly records that no command-run
assertion is made.
\end{enumerate}
Define the finite verifier
\[
V(c,\mathcal C)=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{6}P_i(c,\mathcal C,\Gamma).
\]
If this conjunction is false, the rejected subrecord is the finite set of fields
read by the least-indexed failed predicate.  This paragraph defines the verifier
only; no unprinted semantic oracle is part of it.  The next definition packages
the same finite fields as an explicit input type and a finite acceptance table;
the exactness theorem below then proves that the verifier and the independently
named record-acceptance relation coincide on that finite data structure.

For a package $P$ with ledger $L$, dispatch table $\Gamma$, and submitted
support surface $S$, write
\[
\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)
\]
for the following finite predicate.  For every acceptance entry of a
load-bearing claim $c$ in $L$, there is a publication certificate
$\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$ such that $E\subseteq S$, $V(c,\mathcal C)=1$,
each pass record in $R$ is a submitted row of $L$, the row decision is passing,
and the row's evidence pointer denotes an element of $S$ at the evidence level
recorded in the row.  If $B$ does not contain command, source state,
environment, exit code, and log path, then $\mathsf{RGS}_{S}$ asserts only this
finite row-and-pointer property and asserts no implementation-level correctness
of the gate that produced the row.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Record-gate soundness external interface]
\label{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface}
The external record-gate interface for a package is the finite predicate
\[
\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)
\]
with the following inputs and outputs.  Its inputs are exactly a package $P$, a
submitted support surface $S$, a finite acceptance ledger $L$, and the deterministic
claim-kind dispatch table $\Gamma$ of
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}.  Its positive output is the
finite family of certificates
\[
\{\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B): c\in L
\text{ is an accepted load-bearing claim}\}
\]
such that $E\subseteq S$, $V(c,\mathcal C(c))=1$, every assigned gate in
$\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$ has a submitted passing row in $R$, each evidence
pointer in such a row denotes an element of $S$ at the recorded evidence level,
the human-promotion coordinate $H$ is present exactly at an intake-to-publication
crossing, and the discovery and command-boundary coordinates $D$ and $B$ have the
presence conditions required by the verifier predicates $P_5$ and $P_6$.  Its
negative output, when the predicate fails for a ledger row, is only the finite bad
subrecord read by the first failed verifier predicate among $P_1,\ldots,P_6$.

This interface makes no further claim.  In particular it does not assert that an
external venue accepted the work, that a command was freshly rerun, that a Lean
development builds, that an artifact has the semantic property named in prose, or
that an advisory agent output is proof evidence, unless the corresponding source
path, command, source state, environment, exit code, log path, or gate row is one
of the finite coordinates read above.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Record-gate interface equivalence and non-claim boundary]
\label{prop:record-gate-soundness-external-interface-exactness}
For the operational acceptance interface of
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}, the standalone interface in
Definition~\ref{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface} is equivalent to the
predicate $\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)$ used in the publication-safety theorem.
Moreover, under this interface every consequence used by
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} is determined by the finite
coordinates $(P,S,L,\Gamma)$ and by the displayed certificates
$\mathcal C(c)$; no implementation-level, semantic, venue-acceptance,
fresh-execution, or advisory-evidence claim follows from record-gate soundness
unless it appears as one of those finite coordinates.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The predicate $\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)$ in
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface} quantifies over exactly the
accepted load-bearing ledger entries $c\in L$ and requires exactly the following
data: a certificate $\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$, containment $E\subseteq S$,
acceptance by the finite verifier $V$, submitted passing rows for the pass records
in $R$, and evidence pointers denoting elements of $S$ at the recorded evidence
levels.  The verifier definition expands $V(c,\mathcal C(c))=1$ into the six
predicates $P_1,\ldots,P_6$, which are precisely the kind, evidence-surface,
assigned-gate, human-promotion, discovery, and command-boundary presence
conditions listed in
Definition~\ref{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface}.  Hence a positive
instance of one definition supplies a positive instance of the other, with the
same certificates and the same submitted rows; conversely the standalone
interface supplies every finite field required by the original predicate, so the
original predicate holds.

For the boundary statement, inspect the fields read by $P_1,\ldots,P_6$.  They
contain claim kinds, evidence levels, finite evidence pointers, human-promotion
markers, assigned gate rows, discovery rows, and the command-run or no-command-run
boundary.  None of these fields is a venue decision, a proof-assistant semantic
judgment, an artifact semantic evaluator, a live daemon state, or an advisory
agent proof object unless an additional row explicitly records that fact as an
evidence coordinate satisfying the same verifier predicates.  Therefore the
interface can be used in Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} only to
construct finite publication certificates and finite bad subrecords.  Any stronger
reading would require an added coordinate and is outside the predicate just proved
equivalent.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Stronger-reading preorder]
\label{def:stronger-reading-preorder}
Fix the dispatch table $\Gamma$ and certificate tuple
$\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$ of
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}.  A proposed reading
$c^{+}$ of the same prose claim is \emph{record-stronger} than $c$, written
$c\preceq_{\Gamma} c^{+}$, if at least one of the following finite coordinates is
strictly enlarged while the others are unchanged or enlarged: the claim-kind set
$K$; the evidence level, from $\mathsf{path}$ to $\mathsf{command}$; the evidence
surface $E$; the promotion field $H$; the assigned gate-pass family $R$; the
discovery-interface field $D$; or the boundary field $B$.  It is
\emph{properly stronger} if the enlargement is strict in at least one coordinate.
It is \emph{accepted as a stronger reading} only when the enlarged tuple still
satisfies $\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}$: every newly added kind has its assigned gates,
every newly raised evidence level has the command tuple required by A6, and every
new evidence item is on the submitted support surface or in an explicit
historical boundary.

The preorder is intentionally syntactic and finite-record based.  It is not a
semantic entailment relation between mathematical propositions, and it does not
turn an inventory entry, digest row, or path reference into proof evidence for a
theorem-like environment.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Certificate-schema acceptance table]
\label{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}
Fix the seven-kind dispatch table $\Gamma$ of
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}.  The verifier input for a
load-bearing claim $c$ is the following finite record type:
\[
\mathcal C(c)=
\{K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B\}.
\]
Its typed fields are these and only these: $K$ is a finite set of claim kinds;
$\ell\in\{\mathsf{path},\mathsf{command}\}$ is the declared evidence level; $E$
is a finite evidence-surface list; $H$ is the route-boundary field; $R$ is a
finite family of gate pass records, each with gate name, input surface, decision,
evidence pointer, and evidence level; $D$ is either empty or a finite list of
pinned discovery source-interface rows; and $B$ is the command-boundary field.
This is a data schema, not a theorem: all later exactness claims must be proved
from this displayed record and cannot appeal to unlisted package state.  Define
$\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}(c,\mathcal C)$ to be the conjunction of the six finite
table predicates A1--A6 displayed below; no clause may query generated
prose, a live daemon, an artifact evaluator, a proof assistant, or a venue state
outside these fields and their cited submitted surfaces.

\begin{center}
\centering
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{p{0.08\linewidth}p{0.22\linewidth}X}
\toprule
Clause & Field read & Acceptance condition \\
\midrule
A1 & $K,\ell$ & $K$ is a nonempty subset of the seven-kind domain and $\ell$ is one of the two evidence levels. \\
A2 & $E$ & Every evidence item is a submitted path, review-bundle path, manifest row, log path, pinned commit, or explicit historical-evidence boundary available at level $\ell$. \\
A3 & $K,R,E$ & For every gate in $\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$, $R$ has a pass record with gate name, input surface, decision, evidence pointer, and evidence level; the pointer lies in $E$ or in a named submitted table, manifest row, bundled snapshot, or historical boundary. \\
A4 & $H$ & $H$ records human promotion exactly when $c$ crosses the intake-to-publication boundary, and otherwise records that no such crossing occurs. \\
A5 & $K,D$ & $D$ is present exactly when discovery lies in $K$, and then names the pinned discovery-gate source-interface rows used by $c$. \\
A6 & $\ell,B$ & $B$ records command, source commit, environment, exit code, and log path for command-run evidence, or records that no command-run assertion is made. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Finite certificate schema verifier as a record check]
\label{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier}
For every load-bearing manuscript claim $c$ whose component kinds lie in the
seven-kind domain, $V(c,\mathcal C)$ is a finite, prose-insensitive
record-schema verifier for the declared publication certificate.  The submitted
package instantiates $V$ by the script
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}.  On the positive package
input it reads the schema file, pass-record ledger, submission-interface map,
primary-claim inventory, and review-bundle manifest named above, and outputs
acceptance in \path{review_bundle/certificate_verification_run.log}.  On the
negative fixture \path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example.json} it
outputs rejection in \path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example_run.md}
with the bad subrecord \path{neg1: command-run fields incomplete}.  If $V$
accepts, then $\mathcal C(c)$ is locally checkable at exactly its declared
evidence level.  This is certificate-record verification only: it checks finite
record shape, dispatch, evidence pointers, and command-boundary discipline, not
end-to-end external semantic validation of the referenced artifacts or source
theory.  If $V$ rejects, then a reviewer can extract a finite bad subrecord
consisting of the first failed schema check and the fields on which that check
depends.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The verifier inspects only the displayed tuple $\mathcal C(c)$, the finite
seven-kind domain, the finite dispatch table $\Gamma$, and the finite list of
paths, rows, commits, or logs cited by the record.  The package script realizes
these inputs as five finite JSON files: the schema supplies the seven-kind
domain, evidence levels, gate dispatch table, required record fields, required
pass-record fields, accepted decisions, manifest-required files, and accepted
artifact-prefix patterns; the ledger supplies the seven current records $a_1,
\ldots,a_7$; the submission-interface map and primary-claim inventory supply the
finite manuscript-facing row sets; and the manifest supplies the submitted bundle
paths.  The script output is either the pass transcript in
\path{certificate_verification_run.log} or a list of \path{ERROR: ...} lines,
each naming the failed finite condition.  Since $K$ is required to be a subset of
the seven-kind domain, the set \(\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)\) is finite.  The
checks over $E$, $R$, and the command-run fields are therefore finite lookups in
named manuscript or bundle surfaces, not semantic judgments about generated
prose.

If all six checks pass, the tuple has exactly the fields required in the
definition of a publication certificate: a finite kind set $K$, declared evidence
level $\ell$, evidence surface $E$, route-boundary field $H$, gate-record field
$R$, optional discovery field $D$ with the required presence condition, and
evidence-strength boundary $B$.  Since $V$ is the conjunction of the explicit
predicates $P_1,\ldots,P_6$, the six table clauses A1--A6 hold for exactly the
same finite inputs.  Check~(ii) is precisely the local-checkability condition on
cited paths, manifest rows, logs, pinned commits, and historical boundaries.
Checks~(iii)--(vi) prevent record-level escalations of the declared level:
missing gate-passage records, unrecorded route promotion, unsupported discovery
routing, and command-run assertion without command-run fields.  Thus an accepted
record is locally checkable at the level $\ell$ it declares, and no stronger
level is silently inferred by the record checker.

If a check fails, the verifier stops at a finite test.  The bad subrecord is the
finite collection of fields read by that failed test: for example, $K$ and
$\Gamma$ for a gate-domain failure, $E$ for an evidence-surface failure, $K$,
$\Gamma$, $R$, and the cited evidence item for a gate-record failure, $H$ for a
promotion failure, $K$ and $D$ for a discovery-field failure, or $\ell$ and $B$
for an evidence-level failure.  The supplied negative fixture is exactly the last
case: it declares $\ell=\mathsf{command}$ but provides no
\path{boundary.command_run_fields} containing command, source commit,
environment, exit code, and log path, so the bad subrecord is the pair consisting
of the evidence-level field and the boundary field.  This subrecord is
independent of any surrounding paragraph that may have described the claim.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Verifier input--predicate--output contract]
\label{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}
The verifier interface used by the publication-safety theorem is exactly the
following finite mathematical-software contract.  Its input is a pair
$(c,\mathcal C(c))$, where $c$ is a load-bearing manuscript claim and
\(\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\) is the certificate record of
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}, together with the fixed
dispatch table $\Gamma$ and the submitted support surface $S$.  Its checked
predicates and outputs are:
\begin{center}
\centering
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{p{0.12\linewidth}p{0.30\linewidth}X}
\toprule
Check & Predicate actually checked & Possible output \\
\midrule
$Q_1$ & $K$ is nonempty, $K\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$, and $\ell\in\{\mathsf{path},\mathsf{command}\}$ & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad fields $K,\ell$ \\
$Q_2$ & each item of $E$ is on $S$ or in an explicitly declared pinned, logged, or historical boundary & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad field $E$ and the offending pointer \\
$Q_3$ & every gate assigned by $\Gamma$ to a kind in $K$ has a passing five-field record in $R$ whose pointer is reviewable through $E$, a submitted table, a manifest row, a bundled snapshot, or an historical boundary & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad fields $K,R,E$ and the missing or non-reviewable gate \\
$Q_4$ & $H$ records exactly the human-promotion crossing asserted by $c$, or records that no such crossing is asserted & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad field $H$ \\
$Q_5$ & $D$ is present exactly for discovery-kind claims and then names only pinned source-interface rows used by $c$ & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad fields $K,D$ \\
$Q_6$ & $B$ either contains command, source state, environment, exit code, and log path, or explicitly marks that no command-run assertion is made & accept this coordinate, or reject with bad fields $\ell,B$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
