The positive output is only the finite acceptance bit
$\mathsf{accept}(c,\mathcal C)$ for these six predicates.  The negative output is
only the finite bad subrecord belonging to the first failed predicate.  In
particular, the contract has no output channel for proof-assistant validity,
daemon correctness, artifact semantics, fresh case-study reruns, venue upload, or
venue acceptance.  Therefore any theorem in this paper that invokes the verifier
can certify exactly finite record acceptance for the submitted schema and cannot
certify end-to-end workflow correctness unless an enlarged input schema and an
additional proved verifier contract are supplied.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The input type is displayed in
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}: after the fixed table
$\Gamma$ is chosen, the only variable fields are $K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B$.  The
submitted support surface $S$ is used only to decide whether the finite pointers
named in those fields are reviewer-visible at the declared level.  Thus every
listed predicate is a finite Boolean predicate on the displayed input fields and
on the fixed submitted support surface.

For each row, the predicate is identical to one of the six predicates used in the
definition of $V$: $Q_i=P_i$ for $1\leq i\leq 6$, with the same fields read and
with the same failure subrecord, namely the fields read by the least-indexed
failed predicate.  Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier}
proves that the bundled Python checker is an implementation witness for this
finite schema: it reads the schema, pass-record ledger, submission-interface map,
primary-claim inventory, and manifest, and returns either the pass transcript or
finite error records.  The theorem here is the abstract verifier contract behind
that implementation witness, so its output alphabet is precisely
\(\{\mathsf{accept}\}\) together with the finite bad-subrecord reports.

The final boundary follows by inspection of the input and output alphabets.  No
field in $\mathcal C(c)$ contains an object-language proof derivation, a theorem
prover kernel trace, a daemon transition semantics, an artifact evaluator
semantics, a fresh rerun transcript for the historical case studies, or a venue
receipt.  No output of the contract returns such an object either.  A conclusion
depending on one of those objects is therefore not a function of the declared
input record.  It can become checkable only after the schema is enlarged to add
that object as an input coordinate and after a new verifier contract for the
enlarged coordinate is proved.  Hence the present verifier certifies only finite
record acceptance and its finite rejection witnesses.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Executable-record verifier normal form]
\label{thm:finite-verifier-normal-form}
Fix the dispatch table $\Gamma$, the submitted support surface $S$, and the
finite certificate schema of Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}.
There is a deterministic verifier
\[
\mathsf{Check}_{\Gamma,S}(c,K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)
\]
with the following presentation-layer input/output normal form.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{p{0.18\linewidth}p{0.36\linewidth}X}
\toprule
Step & Input fields read & Returned value \\
\midrule
1 & $K,\ell$ & reject with $(Q_1,K,\ell)$, or continue \\
2 & $E$ and $S$ & reject with $(Q_2,E,e_0)$ for the first non-reviewable pointer $e_0$, or continue \\
3 & $K,\Gamma,R,E,S$ & reject with $(Q_3,g,K,R,E)$ for the first missing or non-reviewable assigned gate $g$, or continue \\
4 & $H$ and the route-crossing status of $c$ & reject with $(Q_4,H)$, or continue \\
5 & $K,D$ & reject with $(Q_5,K,D)$, or continue \\
6 & $\ell,B$ & reject with $(Q_6,\ell,B)$, or return $\mathsf{accept}(c,\mathcal C)$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The verifier is an implementation-contract normal form for this finite schema:
as a map from the displayed finite input record to the displayed finite output
alphabet, it is extensionally equal to the predicate $V$ and to the table
relation $\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}$.  It returns acceptance if and only if all six
schema predicates A1--A6 hold, and otherwise returns exactly the first finite
bad subrecord.  This contract is exhausted by the finite record projection: any
script or implementation that claims to witness this theorem witnesses only the
same input--predicate--output relation on the submitted fields.  In particular,
every positive output used by Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} is
produced by this finite loop over submitted coordinates; the loop has no branch
that can accept a stronger semantic, implementation, venue, archive, or
proof-assistant reading.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Define \(\mathsf{Check}_{\Gamma,S}\) by the six displayed steps.  The first step
is predicate $Q_1$, hence predicate $P_1$ and clause A1.  If it fails, the only
fields inspected are $K$ and $\ell$, so the returned subrecord is finite and is
the first possible failed predicate.  If it succeeds, the verifier proceeds to
step~2.  The second step is exactly the evidence-surface predicate $Q_2=P_2$ and
clause A2; since $E$ is a finite list in an accepted certificate candidate and
$S$ is a finite submitted support surface for this package, membership and
boundary tests are finite lookups, and the first offending pointer $e_0$ is a
finite subrecord.  The third step computes the finite assigned-gate set
\(\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)\) and applies $Q_3=P_3$, clause A3, to the finite
pass-record list $R$ and the finite reviewable surfaces.  A missing assigned
gate or non-reviewable pass-record pointer therefore yields the displayed finite
subrecord and no semantic countermodel.

The remaining steps are the same identification for the remaining coordinates:
step~4 is $Q_4=P_4$ and A4, step~5 is $Q_5=P_5$ and A5, and step~6 is
$Q_6=P_6$ and A6.  Thus a run reaches the accepting branch exactly when none of
the six schema predicates fails.  This proves extensional equality with
\(V=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{6}P_i\), and Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}
identifies the same conjunction with \(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}\).  The bad
subrecord is first-failure exact by construction, because the verifier stops at
the least numbered failed step and has inspected only the fields listed in that
row.

Finally, the normal form lists every input coordinate and every output token of
the algorithm.  This is the promised implementation contract: changing an
implementation while preserving the same finite input projection and the same
six comparisons preserves the accepted and rejected output records, and changing
any accepted output requires changing one of the displayed finite predicates or
one of the displayed input fields.  None of those fields is a proof-assistant
derivation, a theorem-body semantic judgment, an artifact evaluator trace, a
live upload receipt, an archive locator, or a venue decision.  Therefore the
positive branch cannot be read as any of those stronger assertions.  Such an
assertion can be accepted only by enlarging the schema with the missing
coordinate and proving a new input/output contract, as required later by
Theorem~\ref{thm:implementation-soundness-extension-criterion}.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Schema-relative executable-record acceptance exactness]
\label{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}
Fix the seven-kind dispatch table $\Gamma$, the two evidence levels, and the
typed certificate tuple
$\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$ of
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}.  For every
load-bearing claim $c$ and every finite candidate certificate $\mathcal C(c)$,
the finite verifier $V$ of
Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract} accepts $\mathcal C(c)$ if
and only if the six-clause table predicate
$\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}(c,\mathcal C)$ holds.  This is a schema-relative and
finite-record-relative assertion: both sides read exactly the fields named in the
fixed schema and in the finite submitted record surface, and neither side ranges
over unrecorded semantic facts about the referenced mathematics, artifacts, or
gate implementations.  Moreover, if $V$ accepts a record for $c$, the same record
cannot certify any stronger reading $c^{+}$ that adds a new claim kind, a stronger
evidence level, an omitted route crossing, an omitted discovery interface, an
omitted pass record, or a command-run assertion: such a reading has a finite bad
subrecord of the corresponding clause.  Thus $V$ is exact for this paper's finite
record-acceptance relation over the submitted source, tables, manifests, logs,
and explicitly declared historical boundaries, while remaining silent about
external semantic validity of the referenced theory or artifacts and about the
implementation-level soundness of any deployed gate implementation beyond the
recorded pass rows.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The definition of $V$ and the six clauses above use the same finite inputs.
Predicate $P_1$ is clause~A1, $P_2$ is clause~A2, and $P_3$ is clause~A3,
including both assigned-gate coverage and the requirement that each evidence
pointer be tied to the declared review surface.  Predicates $P_4$, $P_5$, and
$P_6$ are exactly clauses~A4, A5, and A6.  Therefore, if
$\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}(c,\mathcal C)$ holds, every verifier predicate is true
and $V$ accepts.

Conversely, suppose $V$ accepts.  Reading the successful checks in order gives
precisely clauses~1--6: the kind set and evidence level are in the permitted
domains; the evidence surface is reviewer-available at the declared level; every
gate assigned by $\Gamma$ has a pass record with the required five fields and an
allowed pointer; promotion and non-promotion are separated by $H$; discovery-kind
claims and only discovery-kind claims carry $D$; and $B$ fixes the command-run
boundary.  Hence $\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}(c,\mathcal C)$ holds.

For the final assertion, keep the accepted tuple fixed and consider a proposed
stronger reading $c^{+}$.  If $c^{+}$ adds a claim kind, the finite set of gates
assigned by $\Gamma$ grows unless the added kind is vacuous in this interface;
without the corresponding pass records clause~3 fails, and if the kind lies
outside the seven-kind domain clause~1 fails.  If $c^{+}$ raises the evidence
level to command-run, clause~6 fails unless the command, commit, environment,
exit code, and log path are present.  If it adds a route crossing, clause~4 fails
unless $H$ contains the human-promotion record.  If it adds discovery content,
clause~5 fails unless the pinned discovery rows are named.  If it asserts a new
gate-dependent conclusion without a pass record, clause~3 fails.  In each case
the failed clause reads only finitely many fields, so deleting irrelevant unread
fields gives an inclusion-minimal bad subrecord.  If none of the clauses changes,
then $c^{+}$ is not stronger in the record semantics: the certificate still
licenses only the same kind set, same evidence level, same gate records, same
route boundary, same discovery interface, and same command boundary.\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Executable schema exactness and negative fixture]
\label{cor:executable-schema-exactness-negative-fixture}
Under the hypotheses and fixed schema of
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, the executable verifier instance
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py} is a finite witness of the
same schema-relative relation, not an additional semantic oracle.  On the positive
submitted package it can witness exactly the records satisfying
\(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}\) at their declared evidence levels.  On the negative
fixture
\path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example.json}, which upgrades a
route record to command-run strength while omitting the command, source state,
environment, exit code, and log path, the extracted bad subrecord is exactly the
evidence-level declaration together with the incomplete boundary field.  Hence
the executable example separates the verifier contract from any stronger reading:
changing prose or ambient pipeline state while keeping the finite schema fields
fixed cannot change either the acceptance bit or the first failed finite
subrecord.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier} identifies the script
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}, the schema files it reads,
and the two recorded runs.  Its positive run returns the finite acceptance
transcript for the submitted records, and its negative run returns the reported
finite error \path{neg1: command-run fields incomplete}.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}, those are the only two output
kinds of the verifier contract: acceptance of the six predicates or a bad
subrecord for the first failed predicate.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, positive acceptance is equivalent
to \(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}\) for the same tuple
\((K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\).  In the negative fixture, clauses A1--A4 and the
non-discovery discipline are supplied for the route row, while the declared
command-run level triggers clause A6 and the required five command-run fields are
absent.  The first failed predicate therefore reads exactly \(\ell\) and \(B\),
so deleting any unrelated prose, score, route history, or ambient implementation
state leaves the same failure.  The script is thus an executable finite-record
witness for the schema-relative theorem and for its negative certificate example,
not evidence of an unrecorded semantic rerun or gate-implementation proof.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-coordinate determination and trust-boundary classifier]
\label{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}
Fix the dispatch table $\Gamma$ and the verifier $V$.  Let $F_{\Gamma}$ be the
finite schema projection read by $V$ from a certificate record, namely the
claim-kind set, declared evidence level, evidence surface, promotion field,
gate-pass rows, discovery-source rows, command-boundary tuple, and the assigned
gate set determined by $\Gamma$.  If two ambient pipeline or artifact states
induce the same values of all coordinates in $F_{\Gamma}$ for a claim $c$, then
they induce the same record-acceptance decision and the same finite publication
certificate for $c$.  Therefore the exactness of
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} is exactness only relative to this
finite schema projection.  No property depending on a coordinate outside
$F_{\Gamma}$---for example the semantic truth of a referenced theorem, truthful
production or implementation soundness of a gate row, a fresh Lean build result,
a daemon trace, an artifact semantic fact, a theorem-environment completeness
claim, or an external venue decision---is a consequence of the accepted
certificate unless that property is first encoded as an additional finite
certificate coordinate and the enlarged record is accepted.  In particular,
record-gate soundness is a load-bearing hypothesis for
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}; it is not supplied by schema
exactness alone.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The verifier $V$ is defined as an ordered list of six checks, and each check reads
only one of the coordinates listed in $F_{\Gamma}$ together with the fixed finite
dispatch table $\Gamma$.  If two ambient states induce the same values on
$F_{\Gamma}$, then the input tuple \((K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\), the assigned gate set,
and every submitted pointer queried by $V$ are identical in the two states.  Each
of the six checks therefore has the same truth value in the two states, so $V$
either accepts both records or rejects both records at the same first failed
finite field.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, an accepted
decision is equivalent to the same six-clause record-acceptance semantics, hence
the finite certificate determined by those clauses is also the same.  This proves
the schema-relative and finite-record-relative factorisation.

Now let $Q$ be a property whose truth can vary while all coordinates in
$F_{\Gamma}$ are held fixed.  Then there exist two ambient states with the same
certificate tuple and the same verifier decision but different truth values for
$Q$.  A conclusion whose truth changes under such an $F_{\Gamma}$-preserving
substitution is not determined by the accepted certificate.  To make $Q$
load-bearing, the operational interface must add a finite coordinate recording
the required evidence: for a build or daemon trace this is a command-run tuple;
for an artifact semantic fact this is an artifact pass record or fresh evaluator
rerun log; for theorem-environment completeness this is an extractor command,
source state, environment, exit code, and log path; and for a venue decision this
is an external evidence surface and the corresponding route/submission record.
Without that enlargement, Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} supplies
only the original finite-record conclusion.

Finally, the same argument isolates record-gate soundness.  The predicate
$\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)$ says that recorded ledger entries actually contain
the assigned passing rows and submitted evidence pointers.  A package can agree
on the schema shape of a candidate certificate while differing on whether such a
record-gate-sound ledger has been supplied.  Thus schema exactness checks the
finite acceptance relation after the row data are present; it does not by itself
make those rows available, truthfully produced, or semantically adequate.
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} therefore states record-gate
soundness as an explicit hypothesis, and the trust boundary is exactly the finite
projection $F_{\Gamma}$ plus that hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Record-interface rigidity and refresh fixed point]
\label{thm:record-interface-rigidity}
Fix a package $P$, submitted support surface $S$, finite ledger $L$, dispatch
table $\Gamma$, and certificate tuple
$\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$ for each accepted load-bearing claim
$c\in L$.  Let $\pi_{\Gamma,S}(P,L)$ be the finite projection consisting of the
tuple fields $(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$, the gate-pass rows in $R$, the submitted
support-surface membership relation for $S$, and the fixed dispatch table
$\Gamma$.  Then the following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The ledger satisfies the external record-gate interface
\(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\) and the verifier accepts every displayed
certificate.
\item The finite projection $\pi_{\Gamma,S}(P,L)$ is a fixed point of the six
schema checks: re-reading the projected rows, evidence pointers, gate assignments,
promotion field, discovery field, and command-boundary field reconstructs the
same family of certificates and produces no new accepted coordinate.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover this fixed point is rigid.  Any proper stronger reading of an accepted
claim---including gate-implementation soundness, fresh command rerun success,
artifact-semantic validity, semantic theorem-inventory completeness, upload
status, or venue acceptance---is accepted by the same interface if and only if it
adds a nonempty finite extension coordinate to
$(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$ or to $R$ and the enlarged projection again satisfies the
fixed-point condition.  In particular, after a submitted support file used by the
projection is regenerated, the current-package finite-record claim is restored
only by the finite refresh sequence that regenerates the dependent theorem
environment inventory, certificate-verifier log, source-interface-verifier log,
primary-claim verifier log, and final digest surface so that all paths and digest
rows are again read from one common projection.  Without that common projection,
the TeX theorem chain retains only its source-level statements and not a
machine-readable current-package exactness claim.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume first that \(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\) holds and that every displayed
certificate is accepted.  By
Proposition~\ref{prop:record-gate-soundness-external-interface-exactness}, the
interface supplies exactly the certificates whose evidence surfaces lie in $S$,
whose assigned gate rows are present and passing, whose evidence pointers denote
submitted elements at the recorded evidence level, and whose promotion,
discovery, and command-boundary fields satisfy the verifier predicates.  Applying
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} to each row identifies this with
the six-clause relation $\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}$.  Hence re-reading precisely the
fields in $\pi_{\Gamma,S}(P,L)$ repeats the same six checks and returns the same
certificate family; no seventh field or stronger coordinate is read by any check.
This is the stated fixed point.

Conversely suppose the finite projection is such a fixed point.  The fixed-point
condition states, field by field, that the kind and evidence-level fields pass
the domain check, that every evidence pointer lies on $S$, that every assigned
gate in $\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$ has a passing row in $R$, that the promotion
coordinate is present exactly when required, and that the discovery and
command-boundary coordinates satisfy the presence conditions of $P_5$ and $P_6$.
These are exactly the positive-output clauses of
Definition~\ref{def:record-gate-soundness-external-interface}.  Therefore
\(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\) holds, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} gives verifier acceptance for the
same displayed certificates.

It remains to prove rigidity.  Let $c^{+}$ be a proper stronger reading of an
accepted claim.  By Definition~\ref{def:stronger-reading-preorder}, being proper
means that at least one of the finite coordinates $K$, $\ell$, $E$, $H$, $R$,
$D$, or $B$ is strictly enlarged.  If the enlarged tuple and any added pass rows
again satisfy the six schema checks, the preceding equivalence applied to the
enlarged projection accepts $c^{+}$.  Conversely, if $c^{+}$ is accepted by the
same finite interface, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}
forces the acceptance decision to be determined by a finite coordinate read by
the verifier.  Since $c^{+}$ is not record-equivalent to $c$, at least one such
coordinate is new or strictly enlarged; otherwise the two readings would have the
same projection and hence the same bounded certificate meaning.  Thus every
proper stronger reading has positive finite extension budget and no stronger
reading is obtained for free from the original fixed point.

The listed examples are exactly instances of this coordinate test.  Gate
implementation soundness requires implementation source, specification,
environment, command or implementation-gate records, and logs.  Fresh rerun
success requires command, source state, environment, exit code, and log path.
Artifact-semantic validity requires an artifact-semantic evidence surface or
evaluator pass row.  Semantic theorem-inventory completeness requires an
extractor command, source-to-inventory binding, produced inventory, and pass row.
Upload and venue readings require external route, submission, or decision
records.  None is among the old projection unless it is explicitly added.

Finally suppose a file on the submitted support surface is regenerated.  The
membership and digest facts read by $P_2$, the pass-row facts read by $P_3$, and
the command-boundary facts read by $P_6$ then refer to a changed byte coordinate.
The fixed-point implication can be applied again only after the finite surfaces
that mirror that coordinate have been recomputed from the same bytes: the theorem
environment extractor supplies the theorem-inventory coordinate, the certificate
verifier supplies the schema-acceptance coordinate, the source-interface verifier
supplies the imported-source coordinate, the primary-claim verifier supplies the
retained-claim coordinate, and the final digest surface supplies the byte identity
shared by the submitted support relation.  If one of these surfaces is stale, two
ambient states can agree on the printed TeX prose while disagreeing on a byte
coordinate read by the projection, so the machine-readable current-package
exactness conclusion is not determined.  What remains is only the TeX-level
statement already proved independently of that regenerated support surface.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Record-gate implementation overclaim obstruction]
\label{thm:record-gate-implementation-overclaim-obstruction}
The hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} and the fixed
point of Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity} determine finite certificate
construction under the external record-gate interface
\(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\), but they do not determine correctness of any
gate implementation that produced, stored, or displayed the pass rows.  More
precisely, for any accepted claim row $c$, the following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The same finite-record calculus accepts an implementation-soundness reading
for the gates assigned to $c$.
\item The package support surface contains a nonempty implementation coordinate
for those gates: gate source identity, source digest or commit, command line,
runtime environment, input records, output records, exit code, log path, digest
binding to the submitted support surface, and an assigned implementation-gate
pass row.
\end{enumerate}
Without this coordinate, every implementation-soundness reading has a finite bad
subrecord, while the bounded certificate-construction reading remains valid under
record-gate soundness.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} uses
\(\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)\) as an external hypothesis: the required finite
pass rows and evidence pointers are present on the submitted surface.  It then
constructs the certificate tuple read by the schema verifier.  Proposition~\ref{prop:record-gate-soundness-external-interface-exactness}
and Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity} show that the finite projection
contains the certificate fields, the assigned pass rows, the submitted-surface
membership relation, and the dispatch table, and that no implementation source,
runtime, input/output trace, or semantic correctness predicate is read unless it
is included as an additional coordinate.

Suppose first that an implementation-soundness reading is accepted.  If no
implementation coordinate of the displayed form were present, two review states
could agree on every field in the record-gate projection and differ only in the
unread gate implementation: in one state the implementation is correct, and in
another it is faulty or not submitted at all, while the displayed pass-row record
is the same.  The verifier would give the same finite answer on both states.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}, a conclusion that
distinguishes them cannot be determined by the current projection.  Hence the
accepted implementation-soundness reading must add a nonempty implementation
coordinate, and omission of that coordinate is the finite bad subrecord named by
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}.

Conversely, if the displayed implementation coordinate and its assigned pass row
are present, then the implementation reading is no longer a free strengthening.
It is an ordinary extension coordinate in the preorder of
Definition~\ref{def:stronger-reading-preorder}.  The enlarged tuple is accepted
exactly when the six schema checks and the additional assigned implementation
gate row form the fixed point of Theorem~\ref{thm:record-interface-rigidity}.
Thus the theorem proves a sharp dichotomy: the current package gives bounded
finite certificate construction under record-gate soundness, and implementation
soundness appears only after the stated finite support extension.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Machine-readable certificate records]
\label{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records}
For the current presentation package, the finite record checker
$V$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier}, with the
record-acceptance semantics of
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, has an executable record-level
instance over the review bundle, conditional on exact supplemental inclusion of
\path{review_bundle/} in the completed submission with matching final digests, as stated in
Proposition~\ref{audit:review-bundle-availability-condition}.  The schema file
\path{review_bundle/certificate_schema.json}, the pass-record ledger
\path{review_bundle/current_package_pass_records.json}, the primary-interface map
\path{review_bundle/submission_interface_map.json}, the primary-claim inventory
\path{review_bundle/primary_claim_inventory.json}, the verifier script
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}, and the recorded run
\path{review_bundle/certificate_verification_run.log} instantiate the verifier at
the declared evidence levels for entries $a_1,\ldots,a_7$.  The negative fixture
\path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example.json} and recorded run
\path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example_run.md} instantiate the first
failure mode for a forbidden command-run escalation.  Each pass record in the
ledger has the five fields \emph{gate name}, \emph{input surface},
\emph{decision}, \emph{evidence pointer}, and \emph{evidence level}.  The
machine-readable submission-interface map is locked to rows \path{sim1}--\path{sim4}
of Table~\ref{tab:submission-interface-map} by its row identifiers, certificates,
surfaces, and evidence levels; the primary-claim inventory is locked to rows
\path{pc1}--\path{pc12} by its row identifiers, source locations, row classes,
and evidence levels.  These executable locks check the finite record interface;
full semantic equality between prose paraphrases and mathematical meaning,
soundness of the gate implementations, and path completeness beyond the declared
surfaces remain outside the record-check claim and stay at the manual audit
boundary stated in the row classes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
All objects in the statement are finite files in the submitted review bundle, so
the claim is a finite record check.  The manifest
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json} contains top-level fields named
\path{certificate_schema}, \path{current_package_pass_records},
\path{submission_interface_map}, \path{primary_claim_inventory},
\path{verifier_script}, and \path{verifier_run_log}; these fields point to the verifier
bundle files named in the statement.  Thus the executable verifier is not an
implicit prose convention but a submitted artifact surface.

The schema file lists the seven claim kinds, the two evidence levels, the same
gate dispatch $\Gamma$ used in the supplement, and the required pass-record
fields $(g,I,d,e,\ell)$.  The pass-record ledger has exactly seven entries,
$a_1,\ldots,a_7$.  For each entry it records a nonempty claim-kind set, the
path-verified evidence level, a finite evidence surface, the route-boundary or
human-promotion field, pass records, the discovery-interface field, and the
evidence boundary.  Inspecting the seven entries gives the required gate coverage:
$a_1$ has inventory and marker-existence records; $a_2$ has promotion, overlap,
submission-state, and submission-pack records; $a_3$ has promotion, overlap, and
submission-state records; $a_4$ has promotion, overlap, submission-state, and
submission-pack records; $a_5$ has pinned-discovery and theorem-content-delta
records; and $a_6$ has status and limitation-ledger records.  These are exactly
the unions of the gate sets assigned by $\Gamma$ to the claim-kind sets displayed
in Table~\ref{tab:current-acceptance-ledger}.  The only row with discovery kind
is $a_5$, and it is the only row with a non-null discovery-interface field; every
path-verified row carries a boundary saying that no command-run assertion is
made.  The seventh row $a_7$ is the bibliography/venue-freshness traceability and
submission-pack record; it is path-verified in the acceptance ledger, while the
separate command-boundary inventory below records the live-check command log used
for the bounded venue and bibliography freshness assertion.

The script \path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py} implements precisely
these finite checks: required manifest fields, entries $a_1$--$a_7$, kind-domain
membership, evidence-level membership, assigned-gate coverage, the five
pass-record fields, evidence-level agreement, discovery-field discipline, the
path-verified boundary condition, exact row locks for \path{sim1}--\path{sim4}
against the submission-interface JSON fields, and row-class locks for
\path{pc1}--\path{pc12} against the primary-claim inventory locations, statuses,
and evidence levels.  The recorded log
\path{review_bundle/certificate_verification_run.log} records a successful run of
that script over the submitted files and states the boundary that no Lean build,
publication-daemon run, or Rule110 dynamic artifact rerun is being certified.
The negative fixture log
\path{review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example_run.md} records the command
\path{python review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py --negative-example review_bundle/negative_certificate_record_example.json}
and the expected rejection of a route row whose evidence level is raised to
command-run without the required command-run tuple.
Consequently the executable artifact proves exactly the schema-level instance of
$V$ claimed here.  By the schema-relative finite-record exactness of Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, this is also
an instance of the paper's finite record-acceptance semantics: the executable
record accepts exactly the current-package certificates and rejects the
recorded command-run escalation example; hence it supplies no stronger evidence
level or omitted gate semantics.

Finally, \path{review_bundle/submission_interface_map.json} has rows
\path{sim1}--\path{sim4}.  The verifier now rejects a mutation of any row's
primary claim text, certificate list, row-or-manifest surface list, or evidence
level, so the JSON file is an executable finite mirror of the interface table at
that declared field level.  The file
\path{review_bundle/primary_claim_inventory.json} lists the concrete primary-note
assertions as rows \path{pc1}--\path{pc12}; every load-bearing row names a
certificate, row, manifest surface, or bounded-context status and declares an
evidence level, and the verifier locks each row's identifier, source location,
status class, and evidence level.  Thus the primary note has a complete finite
claim inventory at the declared review fields, and no additional primary
load-bearing assertion is licensed by the machine-readable interface.  The proof
does not assert that the script decides semantic paraphrase equality or discovers
undeclared paths; those remain the manual audit obligations represented by the
table rows and by Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-inventory-freshness-criterion}.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Executable record non-escalation]
\label{thm:executable-record-non-escalation}
Let $F$ be the finite field projection read by
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}: manifest-field presence,
entries $a_1$--$a_6$, claim-kind membership, evidence-level membership,
assigned-gate coverage, pass-record fields, evidence-level agreement,
discovery-field discipline, path-verified boundary status, row locks for
\path{sim1}--\path{sim4}, and row-class locks for \path{pc1}--\path{pc12}.  Assuming
the exact-or-archive-equivalent review-bundle support surface of
Proposition~\ref{audit:review-bundle-availability-condition}, among all
assertions about the current review bundle that are functions only of this
projection, Proposition~\ref{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records} proves the
maximal executable claim: the six current-package certificates satisfy the finite
record-acceptance relation of Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} at the
declared path-verified fields under the input--predicate--output contract of
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}, and no accepted row carries a
command-run boundary.  Thus the bundled Python script is an implementation
witness for the finite schema, not a separately formalized verifier-language
semantics.  In particular, the executable verifier cannot certify any strictly
stronger assertion whose truth depends on prose-to-semantics equality,
independent extraction of claims from the manuscript, completeness of paths not
declared in the records, or the external semantics of a Lean development,
publication daemon, artifact suite, evaluator, or venue state.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records}, the
script reads exactly the finite projection $F$ listed in the statement and rejects
mutations of the locked fields.  Hence every executable assertion made by that
script is constant on the equivalence classes of review bundles with the same
projection $F$.  On this quotient, the six ledger entries, their kind sets, their
evidence levels, their assigned gates, their pass-record fields, their discovery
boundaries, and their path-verified non-command boundaries are all fixed.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}, the executable checker has no
other mathematical output channel.  The schema-relative finite-record acceptance exactness
theorem then identifies precisely what this fixed data entails: acceptance of
those six certificates in the finite relation
$\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}$ and rejection of stronger readings that add kinds, raise
the evidence level, omit route or discovery fields, omit pass records, or assert
command-run evidence without the required command tuple.

Now consider any proposed stronger assertion of one of the four types named in
the statement.  Its truth is not determined by $F$.  Prose-to-semantics equality
depends on interpreting manuscript language against mathematical meanings, while
$F$ contains only row identifiers, selected text locks, classes, locations,
certificate lists, surfaces, and evidence levels.  Independent extraction depends
on an extraction procedure over the manuscript, while $F$ contains the submitted
inventory rather than the result of an independently specified extractor.
Completeness of undeclared paths depends on paths outside the finite surfaces
read by the verifier.  External artifact semantics depends on running or
semantically validating objects whose command, source state, environment, exit
code, and log path are not present in the path-verified rows.  Therefore two
packages can agree on $F$ while differing on such a stronger assertion.  Since a
function of $F$ has the same value on both packages, no executable claim proved by
this verifier can certify that stronger assertion.  The maximal executable
record-level claim is exactly the one stated in
Proposition~\ref{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records}.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Executable certificate-construction invariant]
\label{thm:executable-certificate-construction-invariant}
For the submitted review bundle, let $G$ be the union of the finite fields read by
the two executable verifiers
\path{review_bundle/verify_source_interface_record.py} and
\path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py}.  Thus $G$ contains the ten
source-interface table triples, the JSON source-interface records, the pinned
source snapshot path, digest, line count, declared source commit and line ranges,
the six current-package pass-record entries $a_1,\ldots,a_6$, the submission-map
rows \path{sim1}--\path{sim4}, the primary-inventory row locks
\path{pc1}--\path{pc12}, and the two recorded successful verifier logs.  On the
equivalence class of packages with the same projection $G$, the publication-safety
interface has a unique executable finite certificate construction: the imported
discovery-gate interface is the sixteen-row checked source interface of
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency}, the accepted
current-package certificates are exactly the six records of
Proposition~\ref{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records}, and any stronger
reading that changes the source interface, adds an accepted row, raises the
evidence level, adds a command-run assertion, or asserts external semantic or
venue correctness is not determined by $G$ and is rejected at the finite-record
boundary unless new certificate fields and verifier records are supplied.  This
is a finite-record construction invariant: the scripts witness the listed
predicate checks on bundled JSON and table fields, and no claim is made here that
Python itself has been given a separate formal operational semantics.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first executable verifier reads only the manuscript source-interface table,
\path{source_interface_record.json}, the \path{pinned_source_snapshot} object of
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json}, and the bundled source snapshot.
Proposition~\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency} proves that these
fields agree on the ordered sixteen triples, the original path, the source commit, the
sixteen line ranges, the snapshot digest, the snapshot line count, and the containment
of each imported label in its declared range.  Hence every package with the same
projection $G$ has the same executable imported discovery-gate interface, and no
table-only, JSON-only, manifest-only, stale-digest, stale-line-count, or
out-of-range imported row can be accepted inside that projection.

The second executable verifier reads the manifest fields, the certificate schema,
the six pass-record entries, the submission-interface map, and the primary-claim
inventory.  Proposition~\ref{prop:machine-readable-certificate-records} proves
that these fields instantiate the finite verifier $V$ for exactly rows
$a_1,\ldots,a_6$ at the path-verified level, with the displayed gate coverage,
discovery-field discipline, row locks, and non-command boundary.  Therefore every
package with the same projection $G$ has the same six accepted current-package
certificates and the same absence of command-run evidence in those certificates.
The input--predicate--output contract,
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}, says that these are the only
positive outputs available to the certificate checker; the finite-record
exactness theorem,
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, identifies these accepted records
with the record-acceptance relation and rejects stronger readings that require
additional claim kinds, evidence surfaces, promotion fields, discovery fields,
pass rows, or command-run tuples.

Combining the two paragraphs gives the stated uniqueness on the $G$-projection:
the discovery component of any accepted current-package claim is forced through
the same checked ten-row source interface, and the publication component is forced
through the same six checked certificate rows.  Any proposed stronger assertion
listed in the statement either changes one of the finite fields of $G$, in which
case it is not a package in the same equivalence class, or leaves $G$ unchanged,
in which case the two verifiers receive identical inputs and can return only the
same finite-record conclusions.  Such an unchanged projection contains no
coordinate for external theorem truth, proof-assistant correctness, unlogged
daemon execution, artifact semantic validity, or venue acceptance.  Those claims
therefore require an enlarged certificate and additional executable records, and
without them they are outside the accepted finite-record interface.\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Deterministic claim-kind dispatch]
\label{prop:claim-kind-dispatch}
For every load-bearing manuscript claim whose kind lies in the seven-kind domain
above, $\Gamma$ assigns a unique finite set of deterministic gates.  In a
pipeline that is record-gate-sound for acceptance, a mixed load-bearing claim may be
used as accepted work only if its acceptance entry records passage through every
gate in the union assigned to its component kinds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The seven atomic clauses in the definition of $\Gamma$ assign one explicitly named
finite gate set to each atomic claim kind.  Hence an atomic claim has a unique
assigned gate set.  For a mixed claim, the component-kind set is finite because
it is a subset of the seven-kind domain; taking the union of the corresponding
finite gate sets is deterministic and finite.  Record-gate-soundness for acceptance
requires the acceptance entry of a load-bearing claim to record passage through
every gate assigned by $\Gamma$.  If a component gate of a mixed claim were
omitted, then the projection of the claim to that component kind would lack its
assigned pass record.  Such an entry would not be record-gate-sound.  Therefore accepted
mixed claims in a record-gate-sound pipeline must pass every gate in the union.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Mixed-claim projection obstruction]
\label{thm:mixed-claim-projection-obstruction}
Let $c$ be a load-bearing mixed claim with nonempty component-kind set
$K\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$ and submitted candidate certificate
$\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)$.  For a nonempty subset $J\subseteq K$, write
\[
G_J=\bigcup_{k\in J}\Gamma(k)
\]
and let $c_J$ denote the projected reading of $c$ that keeps exactly the
claim-kind components in $J$ and keeps the same declared evidence level,
evidence surface, promotion boundary, and command boundary, except that the
discovery field is required precisely when discovery lies in $J$.  Then the
following are equivalent for the projected reading $c_J$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the finite verifier accepts the projected certificate
$\mathcal C_J=(J,\ell,E,H,R|_{G_J},D_J,B)$, where $R|_{G_J}$ is the restriction of
$R$ to rows whose gate name lies in $G_J$ and $D_J$ is $D$ exactly when discovery
lies in $J$ and is absent otherwise;
\item every gate $g\in G_J$ has in $R$ a pass record with the five required
fields and with an allowed evidence pointer, the retained boundary fields $E$,
$H$, $D_J$, and $B$ satisfy the corresponding schema clauses at level $\ell$, and
$J\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, if an acceptance entry for $c$ records passage only through a
proper gate subset $Q\subset\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$, then every component
subset $J$ with $\neg(G_J\subseteq Q)$ has an inclusion-minimal finite bad subrecord,
namely a missing pass record for some gate in $G_J\setminus Q$.  In particular,
no passage through route, submission, scheduler, artifact, traceability,
formal-source, or discovery gates can certify a mixed claim's remaining component
unless the pass rows assigned to that remaining component are also present.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The projected certificate $\mathcal C_J$ is a certificate of the same schema as
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table}, with claim-kind set
$J$ in place of $K$ and with gate field restricted to the finite set $G_J$.
Because $J\subseteq K\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$, Proposition~\ref{prop:claim-kind-dispatch}
assigns exactly the finite gate set $G_J$ to the projected reading.  Applying the
schema-relative exactness theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness},
to $\mathcal C_J$ gives the equivalence: acceptance is exactly satisfaction of
the six finite schema clauses for the retained fields.  Clause A1 is the domain
condition $J\subseteq\mathsf{Dom}(\Gamma)$ and $\ell\in\mathsf{Lev}$; clause A2
checks the evidence surface $E$; clause A3 checks that each gate in $G_J$ has a
pass record in $R|_{G_J}$ with an allowed pointer; clause A4 checks the retained
promotion boundary $H$; clause A5 checks precisely the retained discovery field
$D_J$; and clause A6 checks the retained command boundary $B$.  These are exactly
the conditions in item~2, so items~1 and~2 are equivalent.

Now suppose the row for the mixed claim records only pass rows with gate names in
a proper subset $Q$ of the full assigned gate set.  Choose a nonempty
$J\subseteq K$ with $\neg(G_J\subseteq Q)$ and choose $g\in G_J\setminus Q$.  The
projected verifier for $c_J$ must check $g$ by clause A3, because $g$ is assigned
to the retained component-kind set $J$.  No row for $g$ is present among the
recorded gate names in $Q$, so clause A3 fails.  The verifier reads only the
finite fields consisting of $J$, $\Gamma$, the absent gate name $g$, and the gate
row field $R$; deleting fields not read by this failed check leaves an
inclusion-minimal bad subrecord of the second obstruction type in
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}.  Since the argument
uses only membership in the assigned gate set, it applies uniformly to each of
the seven atomic gate interfaces.  Thus one component's gate passage cannot be
substituted for another component's missing assigned pass rows in a mixed
load-bearing claim.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Concrete finite-script projection-contract boundary]
\label{thm:concrete-finite-script-implementation-soundness}
Let $S$ be the submitted support surface containing
\path{review_bundle/certificate_schema.json},
\path{review_bundle/current_package_pass_records.json},
\path{review_bundle/submission_interface_map.json},
\path{review_bundle/primary_claim_inventory.json},
\path{source_interface_record.json},
\path{review_bundle/REVIEW_BUNDLE_MANIFEST.json}, the bundled pinned source
snapshot, and \path{theorem_inventory.json}.  Let $\mathcal V$ be the four-script
family consisting of \path{review_bundle/verify_certificate_records.py},
\path{review_bundle/verify_source_interface_record.py},
\path{review_bundle/verify_primary_claim_inventory.py}, and
\path{review_bundle/extract_theorem_environments.py}.
Assume that the submitted package contains, for each script in $\mathcal V$ used
as command-run evidence, its source path, invocation command, source state,
environment, exit code, and log path.  Then the following schema-level finite
projection contract is proved for these named scripts and no others: on the
finite input projection explicitly parsed by the corresponding script, exit code
$0$ is exact for the script's printed finite predicate, and this is the maximal
record consequence available from the command-run row unless the package pays a
separate positive extension budget in the sense of
Theorems~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form} and
\ref{thm:five-axis-upgrade-obstruction}.  Namely,
\path{verify_certificate_records.py} certifies schema-relative certificate-row
coverage and pointer discipline for the current pass-record ledger;
\path{verify_source_interface_record.py} certifies equality of the manuscript
source-interface table with the JSON and pinned-snapshot manifest records;
\path{verify_primary_claim_inventory.py} certifies the primary-claim row shape,
line-span bounds, row-id set, and certificate-surface existence checks; and
\path{extract_theorem_environments.py} certifies syntactic theorem-like
environment extraction and label coverage by \path{theorem_inventory.json}.  This
finite-script projection soundness does not certify mathematical theorem
truth, Lean/BEDC correctness, artifact semantic validity, venue upload,
acceptance, archive availability, verifier-source implementation soundness
beyond the parsed projection, or the operational semantics of Python itself.
Any attempted use of the same exit-code row for one of those stronger readings
is rejected by the finite-record interface with an inclusion-minimal bad
subrecord unless the missing implementation, formal-source, artifact-semantic,
venue, archive, or runtime coordinate and its assigned pass row are submitted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each member of $\mathcal V$ is a finite text file included in the submitted
support surface, and the command-run tuple fixes the invoked script, source
state, environment, exit code, and log path.  Inspect the deterministic input
projection named in the statement.
\path{verify_certificate_records.py} reads the certificate schema, the current
pass-record ledger, the submission-interface map, the primary-claim inventory,
the review-bundle manifest, and the selected source surfaces; its accepted output
is obtained only after checking the finite fields used by the schema-relative
certificate predicates: row identifiers, claim-kind sets, evidence levels,
evidence surfaces, human-promotion fields, assigned pass records, discovery
fields, boundary fields, and evidence-pointer existence.  Therefore exit code
$0$ for this script is exactly the accepted side of the finite row-and-pointer
predicate it prints, because every accepted clause is one of those finite
comparisons and an error is appended when such a comparison fails.  This is not
an appeal to a hidden semantics for the script; it is the declared implementation
contract obtained by projecting the command-run record to the finite fields that
the script reads and prints.

The same argument applies to the other three scripts after replacing the finite
projection.  The source-interface verifier reads the manuscript table, the JSON
source-interface record, and the pinned snapshot manifest; every accepted clause
is an equality, digest, line-count, or line-range comparison among those finite
records.  The primary-claim verifier reads \path{submission_abstract.tex} and
\path{review_bundle/primary_claim_inventory.json}; every accepted clause is a
finite row-shape, row-id, line-span, nonempty-field, or certificate-pointer test.
The theorem-environment extractor reads \path{main.tex} and
\path{theorem_inventory.json}; every accepted clause is a regular-expression
extraction of labelled theorem-like environments followed by membership of those
labels in the finite inventory.  Thus, in each case, the submitted command-run
row is used only through the script's finite input projection and the finite
predicate displayed by its source and log: if the script exits with code $0$, the
printed predicate holds on the parsed finite projection.

No stronger conclusion is used.  The proof never interprets theorem bodies,
checks Lean, evaluates artifacts, contacts an upload system, resolves a public
archive, proves verifier-source implementation soundness beyond the parsed
finite predicates, or gives Python a formal semantics.  Those would require
additional certificate coordinates beyond the source path, command, source state,
environment, exit code, log path, and finite input projection specified here,
and the extension-budget normal form of
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form} lists those coordinates.
Conversely, if such a stronger reading were accepted,
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} would force the corresponding
added coordinate to be present and checked.  If it is absent,
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton} gives the
inclusion-minimal bad subrecord, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:five-axis-upgrade-obstruction} rules out replacing it by a row
from another interface axis.  Consequently this theorem supplies maximal
schema-level projection soundness only for the named finite-record scripts and
only at the finite predicates just proved; it is not a broad
implementation-correctness theorem for the surrounding pipeline or for Python
execution in general.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}[Finite-script projection instantiation boundary]
\label{cor:finite-script-rgs-instantiation-boundary}
Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:concrete-finite-script-implementation-soundness} and suppose the submitted command-run records for the four named scripts have exit code $0$.  Then the only command-run coordinate instantiated for those scripts is the schema-level finite projection coordinate for the predicates printed by those scripts.  Consequently Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} may use those command-run rows to support schema-relative certificate coverage, source-interface table equality, primary-claim inventory shape, and theorem-environment label coverage, but not mathematical proof truth, formal-source correctness, artifact semantic validity, venue upload, archive availability, broad implementation correctness, or acceptance.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:concrete-finite-script-implementation-soundness}, each exit-code-$0$ command-run record supplies source path, invocation command, source state, environment, exit code, log path, and a finite input projection for one named verifier.  The conclusion of that theorem is precisely soundness for the script's printed finite predicate on that projection, not correctness of the script outside the parsed finite projection.  These are exactly the finite command-run fields demanded by the audit boundary of Theorem~\ref{thm:assigned-gate-soundness-audit-boundary}, restricted to the four named scripts and their printed predicates.  Hence the enlarged coordinate is available only for those finite predicates.

Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} with this enlarged coordinate changes only the command-run support available for the listed finite checks.  The finite predicates do not interpret theorem proofs, run Lean or BEDC, evaluate artifacts, contact an upload system, resolve an archive, or read a venue decision.  Such claims would require additional claim kinds, evidence surfaces, pass records, and boundary fields under Theorem~\ref{thm:implementation-soundness-extension-criterion}.  Therefore the instantiated record-gate support has exactly the boundary stated in the corollary.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}[Assigned-gate soundness audit boundary]
\label{thm:assigned-gate-soundness-audit-boundary}
Let $P$ be a finite submitted package with pass-record ledger $L$, dispatch
table $\Gamma$, and submitted support surface $S$.  For a load-bearing claim
$c$, write $G(c)=\bigcup_{k\in K(c)}\Gamma(k)$ for the finite set of gates
assigned to its claim-kind set.  The package may use a ledger row for $c$ as a
sound record-gate witness only through the finite predicate
$\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)$ of Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface}.
Equivalently, the available finite-record strength is exactly the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $g\in G(c)$ the submitted ledger contains a pass row for $g$,
including the gate name, input surface, decision, evidence pointer, and evidence
level;
\item every evidence pointer named by those pass rows denotes a submitted path,
manifest row, theorem/proposition/table label, uploaded bundle file, or explicitly
declared historical boundary at the evidence level stated in the row; and
\item if the manuscript asserts any stronger proposition that the underlying gate
implementation was independently sound, freshly executed, or semantically correct
beyond the recorded finite schema check, the submitted package also contains a
gate-soundness audit tuple for that stronger proposition: source path, command,
source state, environment, exit code, and log path for the assigned gate; for the
four named finite-record scripts this tuple has exactly the scope proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:concrete-finite-script-implementation-soundness}.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, in the absence of the audit tuple in (3), record-gate soundness is
an explicit hypothesis of the publication-safety theorem, not a theorem proved by
the current package.  The finite pass rows prove only the package-level statement
that no accepted ledger claim omits an assigned gate row or its declared
submitted evidence surface.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The dispatch table has finite values by Proposition~\ref{prop:claim-kind-dispatch};
hence $G(c)$ is finite.  A submitted pass-record ledger and support surface are
also finite.  The predicate $\mathsf{RGS}_{S}(P,L,\Gamma)$ therefore expands to a
finite conjunction of row and pointer tests: for each accepted load-bearing claim
it supplies a certificate satisfying $V(c,\mathcal C)=1$, and for each gate name
in $G(c)$ it searches the rows attached to $c$ and compares the finite fields
gate name, input surface, decision, evidence pointer, and evidence level.  If
one of these rows is missing, the certificate contains the second bad subrecord
of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}, namely a missing
pass record for an assigned gate.  If a row is present, the verifier has only the
finite string and path fields carried by that row.

The second condition is exactly the local-checkability condition for the row's
evidence pointer.  A pointer to a path, manifest row, theorem/proposition/table
label, uploaded bundle file, or explicit historical boundary is a coordinate of
the submitted finite record surface.  If the pointer does not denote one of
those finite surfaces at the stated evidence level, the certificate has the first
bad subrecord of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}, a
missing evidence surface.  Thus conditions (1) and (2) are precisely the
submitted-row and submitted-pointer clauses of $\mathsf{RGS}_{S}$, and they are
precisely what the package can verify from $L$ and the submitted surfaces.

The third condition concerns a different claim kind.  External implementation
soundness, fresh execution, and semantic correctness assert facts about the
operation of a gate outside the row's finite fields.  By the evidence-level
definitions and by Theorem~\ref{thm:executable-record-non-escalation}, a
command-result claim is not supplied by a path-verified pass row; it requires a
command tuple with source state, environment, exit code, and log path.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}, two worlds that have
identical submitted pass rows but different unsubmitted gate implementations are
indistinguishable to the finite verifier.  Hence no proof from (1) and (2) can
establish the stronger external-gate proposition.  The stronger proposition is
accepted only after the package is enlarged by the audit tuple in (3).  Without
that enlargement, record-gate soundness remains the explicit finite predicate
$\mathsf{RGS}_{S}$, while the proved package fact is exactly the absence of
missing assigned-gate rows and missing declared evidence surfaces.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite certificate obstruction skeleton]
\label{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton}
Let $c$ be a load-bearing manuscript claim whose component kinds lie in the
seven-kind domain.  The verifier of
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier} gives the following
complete audit classification at the declared evidence level.  A certificate for
$c$ passes the publication-safety interface if and only if it has no bad
subrecord of the following five types:
\begin{enumerate}
\item missing evidence surface for the declared evidence level;
\item missing pass record for a gate in $\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k)$;
\item missing human-promotion record when the claim crosses from intake or
source material into active publication work;
\item discovery escalation without the pinned discovery-gate source-interface
rows for certificate, ledger, lineage, and boundary witnesses; or
\item command-run escalation without command, source commit, environment, exit
code, and log path.
\end{enumerate}
Equivalently, every unsafe accepted claim contains an inclusion-minimal finite
bad subrecord of one of these five types, and deleting all other fields preserves
the same rejection by the verifier.  This is an audit-skeleton theorem for the
software-workshop route: it classifies finite certificate failures and makes no
claim about theorem-proving complexity or about fresh execution of the
underlying workflow.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $K$ be the finite set of component kinds of $c$.  Since $K$ is a subset of
the seven-kind domain, Proposition~\ref{prop:claim-kind-dispatch} gives a unique
finite required gate set
\[
G(c)=\bigcup_{k\in K}\Gamma(k).
\]
The certificate field $R$ is indexed by exactly this finite set, so checking
gate passage is a finite lookup: each $g\in G(c)$ must have a pass record and the
record must cite an item of the finite evidence surface $E$.  The evidence-level
field $\ell$ and boundary field $B$ make the same lookup finite for evidence
strength: at path-verified level the certificate must name the source, artifact,
pinned commit, local publication path, or gate-decision surface used by the
claim; at command-run level it must additionally name command, source commit,
environment, exit code, and log path.  Neither lookup interprets artifact
semantics, reruns Lean, or predicts editorial outcomes; those are stronger claim
kinds or stronger evidence levels that require their own records.  The human-promotion field $H$ separates
the route boundary from all other gates: it is either a concrete promotion
record or a finite marker that no such boundary is crossed.  Finally, when
discovery lies in $K$, the discovery field $D$ names the pinned source-interface
rows certified by Lemma~\ref{lem:source-interface-extraction-correctness} and
Propositions~\ref{prop:reviewable-source-snapshot-interface} and
\ref{prop:source-interface-record-file-consistency}; these rows are finite and
therefore locally checkable in the same way as the other certificate fields.
Thus the certificate is sufficient for a reviewer to check safety by examining a
finite list of fields and cited artifacts, not by trusting generated prose.

It remains to prove that the displayed obstruction list is complete.  Suppose an
accepted claim fails the publication-safety interface.  Choose an inclusion-minimal
subrecord of its acceptance entry that still witnesses failure.  The certificate
schema has exactly seven coordinates, and the first two of them are not extra
obstruction families.  The claim-kind coordinate $K$ only determines the finite
gate set $G(c)$ through $\Gamma$; once $K$ lies in the seven-kind domain, any
failure caused by that determination is a missing assigned gate and hence type~2.
The evidence-level coordinate $\ell$ only chooses between the path-verified and
command-run interpretations of the boundary field; its failures therefore appear
either as absent evidence at the declared level, type~1, or as absent command-run
coordinates, type~5.  Thus every possible failure must be witnessed in one of
the five substantive schema fields $E,R,H,D,B$.

If the failing field is the evidence surface $E$, the claim is not tied to a
submitted path, bundle path, manifest row, log path, pinned commit, or explicit
historical boundary at the declared level.  The minimal subrecord is therefore
type~1.  If the failing field is the gate-record field $R$, some gate in the
deterministic finite set $G(c)$ has no pass record, no decision, or no evidence
pointer lying in the submitted surface; this is type~2.  If the failing field is
the human-promotion field $H$, then all non-route evidence may be present but the
claim has crossed from intake or source material into active publication work
without the required human promotion marker; this is type~3.  If the failing
field is the discovery field $D$, then a discovery or classifier-shift reading is
being inferred from statement-only, code-only, mechanical, compression-only, or
classifier-scored material without the pinned certificate, ledger, lineage, and
boundary rows; this is type~4.  Finally, if the failing field is the boundary
field $B$, the record either raises path-verified evidence to command-run
strength without command, source commit, environment, exit code, and log path, or
omits the explicit no-command-run boundary; this is type~5.

The preceding paragraph exhausts the schema fields: $K$ and $\ell$ reduce to the
same five substantive checks, and $E,R,H,D,B$ are precisely the five displayed
obstruction types.  Minimality removes all unread fields and leaves exactly one
of the five obstruction skeletons.  Conversely, if no bad subrecord of type~1--5
is present, then each schema check in
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-certificate-schema-verifier} succeeds: the evidence
surface is present at the declared level, every required gate has its recorded
pass, the route-boundary field is correct, discovery rows are present exactly
when required, and command-run strength is not asserted without command-run
fields.  Hence the verifier accepts the certificate and the publication-safety
interface holds for $c$ at the declared evidence level.

The final sentence follows from the construction.  The verifier reads only the
finite certificate fields and the finite dispatch table, so the theorem
classifies certificate failures for review.  It does not assert a lower bound,
complexity separation, automated theorem-proving method, or fresh run of any
external command.\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite extension-budget normal form]
\label{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form}
Fix the dispatch table $\Gamma$, the finite certificate schema, and an accepted
finite certificate
\(\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\) for a load-bearing package claim $c$.
Let $c^{+}$ be a proposed stronger reading of the same row which is not
record-equivalent to $c$ in the sense of
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}.  Then $c^{+}$ is
accepted by the publication-safety interface if and only if the submitted finite
record surface contains an extension record in the following normal form:
\[
\Delta(c,c^{+})=(\Delta K,\Delta E,\Delta H,\Delta R,\Delta D,\Delta B),
\]
where the nonempty coordinates are exactly the coordinates needed by the stronger
reading:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\Delta K$ names every additional claim-kind component asserted by
$c^{+}$;
\item $\Delta E$ supplies the submitted evidence surfaces for those added
components at the declared evidence level;
\item $\Delta R$ supplies pass records for every gate in
\(\bigcup_{k\in K\cup\Delta K}\Gamma(k)\) not already supplied by $R$;
\item $\Delta H$ supplies any newly asserted promotion, route, venue, or external
instantiation boundary;
\item $\Delta D$ supplies the pinned discovery-source rows whenever the stronger
reading adds discovery, classifier-shift, or theorem-content-delta content; and
\item $\Delta B$ supplies the command, source-state, environment, exit-code, and
log-path tuple whenever the stronger reading raises a path-verified row to
command-run strength.
\end{enumerate}
Equivalently, every genuine strengthening has positive finite extension budget:
at least one coordinate of \(\Delta(c,c^{+})\) must be nonempty, and every
nonempty obstruction class from
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton} must be paid by the
corresponding coordinate of \(\Delta(c,c^{+})\).  Without this extension record,
the stronger reading has an inclusion-minimal bad subrecord and remains outside
the certificate constructed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First suppose that the extension record is present.  Enlarge the certificate by
replacing its fields with
\[
(K\cup\Delta K,\ell,E\cup\Delta E,H\cup\Delta H,
R\cup\Delta R,D\cup\Delta D,B\cup\Delta B),
\]
where the unions mean finite row union with the original row identifiers retained.
The six clauses of Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table} are
then exactly the six requirements displayed in the statement.  Clause~A1 reads
the enlarged kind set and evidence level.  Clause~A2 reads the enlarged evidence
surface.  Clause~A3 reads the enlarged gate-pass field and checks every gate
assigned by $\Gamma$ to the enlarged kind set.  Clause~A4 reads the enlarged
promotion and boundary field.  Clause~A5 reads the enlarged discovery-interface
field exactly when discovery is present in the enlarged kind set.  Clause~A6
reads the enlarged evidence-boundary field and accepts command-run strength only
when the command tuple is present.  Therefore
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness} gives
\(\mathsf{Acc}_{\Gamma}(c^{+},\mathcal C\cup\Delta)\), and the finite interface
accepts the stronger reading at precisely the declared evidence level.

Conversely, suppose $c^{+}$ is accepted.  Since $c^{+}$ is not
record-equivalent to $c$, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary}
implies that some finite coordinate read by the verifier differs from the
coordinate of \(\mathcal C(c)\).  Collect the differences in the six displayed
components: additional kinds in $\Delta K$, additional submitted surfaces in
$\Delta E$, additional gate rows in $\Delta R$, additional promotion or external
boundary fields in $\Delta H$, additional discovery rows in $\Delta D$, and
additional evidence-boundary or command-run data in $\Delta B$.  These are finite
because the verifier reads only finite records and the dispatch table has finite
domain.  Acceptance of $c^{+}$ forces each changed coordinate to satisfy the
corresponding clause A1--A6, so the collected difference record has the normal
form stated above.

It remains only to prove positivity and obstruction completeness.  If every
coordinate of \(\Delta(c,c^{+})\) were empty, then $c$ and $c^{+}$ would induce the
same values on all verifier coordinates and would be record-equivalent, contrary
to hypothesis.  Thus any genuine strengthening has positive finite extension
budget.  If some nonempty obstruction class is not supplied by its corresponding
coordinate, Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-certificate-obstruction-skeleton} identifies
the first failed finite field as one of the five bad-subrecord types: missing
evidence surface, missing gate record, missing promotion or external boundary,
missing discovery rows, or unsupported command-run escalation.  Removing all
unread fields leaves an inclusion-minimal bad subrecord.  Hence a stronger
reading is accepted exactly when its finite extension budget is paid in submitted
coordinates, and otherwise remains outside the original certificate.\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Dependency normal form for the publication-safety chain]
\label{prop:publication-safety-dependency-normal-form}
Let $c$ be a load-bearing claim whose certificate
\(\mathsf{Cert}_{\Gamma}(c)=\mathcal C(c)=(K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\) is constructed by
the finite schema verifier from the submitted surface $S$, ledger $L$, and
dispatch table $\Gamma$.  Then every use of the publication-safety interface in
the main theorem chain factors through the following ordered dependency graph:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{construction and schema-relative exactness}: the tuple
\((K,\ell,E,H,R,D,B)\) is formed from submitted finite records and is accepted
exactly when clauses A1--A6 of
Definition~\ref{def:certificate-schema-acceptance-table} hold, as proved by
Theorems~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract} and
\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness};
\item \emph{extension-budget necessity and sufficiency}: for any genuinely
stronger reading $c^{+}$ that is not record-equivalent to $c$, acceptance of
$c^{+}$ is equivalent to the presence of the finite extension record
\(\Delta(c,c^{+})=(\Delta K,\Delta E,\Delta H,\Delta R,\Delta D,\Delta B)\) of
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form}; and
\item \emph{maximality}: if the required nonempty extension coordinates are
absent, the original certificate remains accepted for $c$ and is maximal below
$c^{+}$ on the submitted finite surface, while $c^{+}$ is rejected by a finite
bad subrecord as in Proposition~\ref{prop:publication-safety-maximality}.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the sharp ``if and only if'' and maximality language in
Theorem~\ref{thm:publication-safety-interface} is only the composite of these
three finite-record statements.  It is not a separate semantic assertion about
gate implementations, Lean/BEDC source truth, artifact behaviour, daemon runs, or
venue acceptance.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first dependency is the construction part.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:verifier-input-output-contract}, the verifier input consists
only of the submitted finite certificate fields, and its predicates are exactly
the six checks A1--A6.  The schema-relative exactness theorem,
Theorem~\ref{thm:record-acceptance-exactness}, proves the biconditional between
passing those checks and satisfying the record-acceptance semantics for the
dispatch table $\Gamma$.  Thus the publication-safety theorem can construct no
certificate other than the tuple read from the finite surface, and acceptance of
that tuple is exact only relative to the printed schema.

For the second dependency, let $c^{+}$ be a genuinely stronger reading.  If it is
accepted, then it cannot be record-equivalent to $c$, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-coordinate-determination-boundary} supplies at least one
changed finite verifier coordinate.  Collecting the changed kind, evidence,
human-boundary, gate, discovery, and command-boundary coordinates gives exactly
the six-component extension record of
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-extension-budget-normal-form}.  Conversely, if that
extension record is present and its assigned gates pass, the enlarged tuple
satisfies the corresponding clauses A1--A6, so schema-relative exactness accepts
the stronger reading.  This proves both necessity and sufficiency of the
extension budget.

The maximality dependency is then immediate.  If a genuine stronger reading has
no paid extension coordinate, the construction and exactness dependency still
accepts the original tuple for $c$, while the extension-budget theorem says that
some required finite coordinate for $c^{+}$ is missing.  The obstruction skeleton
therefore returns the first failed finite field as a bad subrecord.  This is
precisely the maximality assertion: the accepted record is largest among the
finite consequences supported by the submitted tuple, but it does not cross into
any stronger reading whose extension budget is unpaid.  Since all three steps
quantify only over the verifier's finite coordinates, their composite cannot
certify external semantic or implementation facts that are not among those
coordinates.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Gate-interface separation]
\label{prop:gate-interface-separation}
The promotion, overlap, submission-state, and theorem-content delta gates are
separate deterministic interfaces.  None can be replaced by passage of another
one when a load-bearing claim has the corresponding component.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each named gate has a distinct invariant and evidence surface in the operational
definition.  Promotion asks whether a candidate has crossed the human
publication boundary; its evidence is the active-paper directory and pipeline
metadata.  Overlap asks whether the route duplicates or conflicts with a sibling
route; its evidence is route identity, overlap ledgers, submitted-sibling paths,
and board state.  Submission-state asks whether the proposed publication action
is compatible with submission markers, venue state, and submission-pack metadata.
The theorem-content delta gate asks whether an asserted extension contains
load-bearing theorem growth; its evidence is an inventory, hard-lint surface, or
delta record.  These predicates are logically independent at the interface
level: an active paper can still overlap a submitted sibling, a route with no
overlap can still have an impermissible submission state, and a compiling edit in
a permissible route can still lack theorem-content delta.  Therefore a
load-bearing claim with any one of these components must pass that component's
own gate, and passage through the others cannot supply the missing evidence.\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Human promotion is not daemon-visible active-track equivalence]
\label{cor:human-promotion-daemon-active-track-separation}
In the operational interface of Definition~\ref{def:operational-acceptance-interface},
a human-promotion record and daemon-visible active-paper markers are not
interchangeable certificate fields.  More precisely, let $c$ be any
route-boundary claim whose accepted certificate is
